[Synonyms](/wiki/Synonym_\(taxonomy\) "Synonym \(taxonomy\)")  
Neornithes [Gadow](/wiki/Hans_Friedrich_Gadow "Hans Friedrich Gadow"), 1883  
  
**Birds** are a group of [warm-blooded](/wiki/Warm-blooded "Warm-blooded") [vertebrate](/wiki/Vertebrate "Vertebrate") [animals](/wiki/Animal "Animal") constituting the [class](/wiki/Class_\(biology\) "Class \(biology\)") **Aves** , characterised by [feathers](/wiki/Feather "Feather"), toothless [beaked](/wiki/Beak "Beak") jaws, the [laying](/wiki/Oviparity "Oviparity") of [hard-shelled](/wiki/Eggshell "Eggshell") eggs, a high [metabolic](/wiki/Metabolism "Metabolism") rate, a four-chambered [heart](/wiki/Heart "Heart"), and a strong yet lightweight [skeleton](/wiki/Bird_skeleton "Bird skeleton"). Birds live worldwide and range in size from the 5.5 cm (2.2 in) [bee hummingbird](/wiki/Bee_hummingbird "Bee hummingbird") to the 2.8 m (9 ft 2 in) [common ostrich](/wiki/Common_ostrich "Common ostrich"). There are over 11,000 living species and they are split into 44 [orders](/wiki/Order_\(biology\) "Order \(biology\)"). More than half are [passerine](/wiki/Passerine "Passerine") or "perching" birds. Birds have [wings](/wiki/Bird_wing "Bird wing") whose development varies according to species; the only known groups without wings are the extinct [moa](/wiki/Moa "Moa") and [elephant birds](/wiki/Elephant_bird "Elephant bird"). Wings, which are modified [forelimbs](/wiki/Forelimb "Forelimb"), gave birds the ability to fly, although further evolution has led to the [loss of flight in some birds](/wiki/Flightless_bird "Flightless bird"), including [ratites](/wiki/Ratite "Ratite"), [penguins](/wiki/Penguin "Penguin"), and diverse [endemic](/wiki/Endemism "Endemism") island species. The digestive and respiratory systems of birds are also uniquely adapted for flight. Some bird species of aquatic environments, particularly [seabirds](/wiki/Seabird "Seabird") and some [waterbirds](/wiki/Water_bird "Water bird"), have further evolved for swimming. The study of birds is called [ornithology](/wiki/Ornithology "Ornithology"). 

Birds [evolved](/wiki/Evolution_of_birds "Evolution of birds") from earlier [theropods](/wiki/Theropods "Theropods"), and thus constitute the only known living [dinosaurs](/wiki/Dinosaurs "Dinosaurs"). Likewise, birds are considered [reptiles](/wiki/Reptile "Reptile") in the modern [cladistic](/wiki/Cladistics "Cladistics") sense of the term, and their closest living relatives are the [crocodilians](/wiki/Crocodilia "Crocodilia"). Birds are descendants of the primitive [avialans](/wiki/Avialae "Avialae") (whose members include _[Archaeopteryx](/wiki/Archaeopteryx "Archaeopteryx")_) which first appeared during the [Late Jurassic](/wiki/Late_Jurassic "Late Jurassic"). According to some estimates, modern birds (**Neornithes**) evolved in the [Late Cretaceous](/wiki/Late_Cretaceous "Late Cretaceous") or between the Early and Late Cretaceous (100 Ma) and diversified dramatically around the time of the [Cretaceous–Paleogene extinction event](/wiki/Cretaceous%E2%80%93Paleogene_extinction_event "Cretaceous–Paleogene extinction event") 66 million years ago, which killed off the [pterosaurs](/wiki/Pterosaur "Pterosaur") and all non-[ornithuran](/wiki/Ornithuran "Ornithuran") dinosaurs.[4][5]

Many [social species](/wiki/Social_animal "Social animal") preserve knowledge across generations ([culture](/wiki/Animal_culture#Examples_of_culturally_transmitted_behaviors_in_birds "Animal culture")). Birds are social, communicating with visual signals, calls, and [songs](/wiki/Bird_vocalization "Bird vocalization"), and participating in such behaviour as [cooperative breeding](/wiki/Helpers_at_the_nest "Helpers at the nest") and hunting, [flocking](/wiki/Flocking_\(behavior\) "Flocking \(behavior\)"), and [mobbing](/wiki/Mobbing_\(animal_behavior\) "Mobbing \(animal behavior\)") of predators. The vast majority of bird species are socially (but not necessarily sexually) [monogamous](/wiki/Monogamy_in_animals "Monogamy in animals"), usually for one breeding season at a time, sometimes for years, and rarely for life. Other species have breeding systems that are [polygynous](/wiki/Polygyny_in_animals "Polygyny in animals") (one male with many females) or, rarely, [polyandrous](/wiki/Polyandry_in_nature "Polyandry in nature") (one female with many males). Birds produce offspring by laying eggs which are fertilised through [sexual reproduction](/wiki/Sexual_reproduction "Sexual reproduction"). They are usually laid in a nest and [incubated](/wiki/Avian_incubation "Avian incubation") by the parents. Most birds have an extended period of parental care after hatching. 

Many species of birds are economically important as food for human consumption and raw material in manufacturing, with [domesticated](/wiki/Poultry "Poultry") and [undomesticated](/wiki/Game_\(hunting\) "Game \(hunting\)") birds being important sources of eggs, meat, and feathers. [Songbirds](/wiki/Songbird "Songbird"), parrots, and other species are popular as pets. [Guano](/wiki/Guano "Guano") (bird excrement) is harvested for use as a fertiliser. Birds figure throughout human culture. About 120 to 130 species have become [extinct](/wiki/Bird_extinction "Bird extinction") due to human activity since the 17th century, and hundreds more before then. Human activity threatens about 1,200 bird species with extinction, though efforts are underway to protect them. Recreational [birdwatching](/wiki/Birdwatching "Birdwatching") is an important part of the [ecotourism](/wiki/Ecotourism "Ecotourism") industry. 

## Evolution and classification

Main article: [Evolution of birds](/wiki/Evolution_of_birds "Evolution of birds")

[](/wiki/File:Archaeopteryx_lithographica_\(Berlin_specimen\).jpg)_[Archaeopteryx](/wiki/Archaeopteryx "Archaeopteryx")_ is often considered the oldest known true bird.

The first [classification](/wiki/Biological_classification "Biological classification") of birds was developed by [Francis Willughby](/wiki/Francis_Willughby "Francis Willughby") and [John Ray](/wiki/John_Ray "John Ray") in their 1676 volume _Ornithologiae_.[6] [Carl Linnaeus](/wiki/Carl_Linnaeus "Carl Linnaeus") modified that work in 1758 to devise the [taxonomic classification](/wiki/Taxonomic_classification "Taxonomic classification") system currently in use.[7] Birds are usually categorised as a [biological class](/wiki/Class_\(biology\) "Class \(biology\)") in traditional [evolutionary taxonomy](/wiki/Evolutionary_taxonomy "Evolutionary taxonomy"). Modern [phylogenetic taxonomy](/wiki/Phylogenetic_taxonomy "Phylogenetic taxonomy") places Aves in the [clade](/wiki/Clade "Clade") [Theropoda](/wiki/Theropoda "Theropoda"), sometimes with a Linnean rank lower than class, such as subclass[8] or infraclass.[9]

### Definition

Aves and a sister group, the order [Crocodilia](/wiki/Crocodilia "Crocodilia"), contain the only living representatives of the reptile clade [Archosauria](/wiki/Archosauria "Archosauria"). During the late 1990s, Aves was most commonly defined [phylogenetically](/wiki/Phylogenetics "Phylogenetics") as all descendants of the [most recent common ancestor](/wiki/Most_recent_common_ancestor "Most recent common ancestor") of modern birds and _[Archaeopteryx lithographica](/wiki/Archaeopteryx "Archaeopteryx")_.[10] However, an earlier definition proposed by [Jacques Gauthier](/wiki/Jacques_Gauthier "Jacques Gauthier") gained wide currency in the 21st century, and is used by many scientists including adherents to the _[PhyloCode](/wiki/PhyloCode "PhyloCode")_. Gauthier defined Aves to include only the [crown group](/wiki/Crown_group "Crown group") of the set of modern birds. This was done by excluding most groups known only from [fossils](/wiki/Fossils "Fossils"), and assigning them, instead, to the broader group Avialae,[11] on the principle that a clade based on extant species should be limited to those extant species and their closest extinct relatives.[11]

Gauthier and de Queiroz identified four different definitions for the same biological name "Aves", which is a problem.[12] The authors proposed to reserve the term Aves only for the crown group consisting of the last common ancestor of all living birds and all of its descendants,[12] which corresponds to meaning number 4 below. They assigned other names to the other groups.[12]

| [Reptiles](/wiki/Reptile "Reptile") |  |  |  | [Squamates](/wiki/Squamate "Squamate") |  [Lizards](/wiki/Lizard "Lizard") & [snakes](/wiki/Snake "Snake")  
---|---  
  
|  | [Pantestudines](/wiki/Pantestudines "Pantestudines") |  [Turtles](/wiki/Turtle "Turtle")  
---|---  
[Archosaurs](/wiki/Archosaur "Archosaur") |  |  |  [Crocodiles](/wiki/Crocodile "Crocodile")  
---|---  
|  **Birds**  
  
The birds' phylogenetic relationships to major living reptile groups. (The [turtles](/wiki/Turtle "Turtle")' position is uncertain: Some authorities embed them inside the [Archosaurs](/wiki/Archosaur "Archosaur"), with **birds** and [crocodiles](/wiki/Crocodile "Crocodile").)  
  
  1. Aves can mean all [archosaurs](/wiki/Archosaur "Archosaur") closer to birds than to [crocodiles](/wiki/Crocodile "Crocodile") (alternately [Avemetatarsalia](/wiki/Avemetatarsalia "Avemetatarsalia"))
  2. Aves can mean those advanced archosaurs with feathers (alternately [Avifilopluma](/wiki/Avifilopluma "Avifilopluma"))
  3. Aves can mean those feathered dinosaurs that fly (alternately [Avialae](/wiki/Avialae "Avialae"))
  4. Aves can mean the last common ancestor of all the currently living birds and all of its descendants (a "[crown group](/wiki/Crown_group "Crown group")", in this sense synonymous with **Neornithes**)



Under the fourth definition _Archaeopteryx_ , traditionally considered one of the earliest members of Aves, is removed from this group, becoming a non-avian dinosaur instead. These proposals have been adopted by many researchers in the field of palaeontology and [bird evolution](/wiki/Bird_evolution "Bird evolution"), though the exact definitions applied have been inconsistent. Avialae, initially proposed to replace the traditional fossil content of Aves, is often used synonymously with the vernacular term "bird" by these researchers.[13]

| Maniraptoromorpha  |  |  |  |  |  † _[Coelurus](/wiki/Coelurus "Coelurus")_  
---|---  
  
|  |  |  † _[Ornitholestes](/wiki/Ornitholestes "Ornitholestes")_  
---|---  
[Maniraptoriformes](/wiki/Maniraptoriformes "Maniraptoriformes") |  |  |  †[Ornithomimosauria](/wiki/Ornithomimosauria "Ornithomimosauria")  
---|---  
[Maniraptora](/wiki/Maniraptora "Maniraptora") |  |  |  †[Alvarezsauridae](/wiki/Alvarezsauridae "Alvarezsauridae")  
---|---  
[Pennaraptora](/wiki/Pennaraptora "Pennaraptora") |  |  |  †[Oviraptorosauria](/wiki/Oviraptorosauria "Oviraptorosauria")  
---|---  
|  [Paraves](/wiki/Paraves "Paraves")  
  
Cladogram showing the results of a phylogenetic study by Cau, 2018.[14]  
  
Most researchers define Avialae as branch-based clade, though definitions vary. Many authors have used a definition similar to "all [theropods](/wiki/Theropod "Theropod") closer to birds than to _[Deinonychus](/wiki/Deinonychus "Deinonychus")_ ",[15][16] with _[Troodon](/wiki/Troodon "Troodon")_ being sometimes added as a second external specifier in case it is closer to birds than to _Deinonychus_.[17] Avialae is also occasionally defined as an [apomorphy-based clade](/wiki/Phylogenetic_nomenclature#Phylogenetic_definitions "Phylogenetic nomenclature") (that is, one based on physical characteristics). [Jacques Gauthier](/wiki/Jacques_Gauthier "Jacques Gauthier"), who named Avialae in 1986, re-defined it in 2001 as all dinosaurs that possessed feathered wings used in flapping [flight](/wiki/Bird_flight "Bird flight"), and the birds that descended from them.[12][18]

Despite being currently one of the most widely used, the crown-group definition of Aves has been criticised by some researchers. Lee and Spencer (1997) argued that, contrary to what Gauthier defended, this definition would not increase the stability of the clade and the exact content of Aves will always be uncertain because any defined clade (either crown or not) will have few synapomorphies distinguishing it from its closest relatives. Their alternative definition is synonymous to Avifilopluma.[19]

### Dinosaurs and the origin of birds

Main article: [Origin of birds](/wiki/Origin_of_birds "Origin of birds")

| [Paraves](/wiki/Paraves "Paraves") |  |  |  †[Scansoriopterygidae](/wiki/Scansoriopterygidae "Scansoriopterygidae")  
---|---  
|  |  |  † _[Eosinopteryx](/wiki/Eosinopteryx "Eosinopteryx")_  
---|---  
[Eumaniraptora](/wiki/Eumaniraptora "Eumaniraptora") |  |  |  † _[Jinfengopteryx](/wiki/Jinfengopteryx "Jinfengopteryx")_  
---|---  
|  † _[Aurornis](/wiki/Aurornis "Aurornis")_  
|  †[Dromaeosauridae](/wiki/Dromaeosauridae "Dromaeosauridae")  
|  †[Troodontidae](/wiki/Troodontidae "Troodontidae")  
|  [Avialae](/wiki/Avialae "Avialae")  
  
Cladogram following the results of a phylogenetic study by Cau _et al._ , 2015[20]  
[](/wiki/File:Birds_and_dinosaurs.webp)Simplified [phylogenetic tree](/wiki/Phylogenetic_tree "Phylogenetic tree") showing the relationship between modern birds and other dinosaurs[21]

Based on [fossil](/wiki/Fossil "Fossil") and biological evidence, most scientists accept that birds are a specialised subgroup of [theropod](/wiki/Theropod "Theropod") [dinosaurs](/wiki/Dinosaur "Dinosaur")[22] and, more specifically, members of [Maniraptora](/wiki/Maniraptora "Maniraptora"), a group of theropods which includes [dromaeosaurids](/wiki/Dromaeosauridae "Dromaeosauridae") and [oviraptorosaurs](/wiki/Oviraptorosauria "Oviraptorosauria"), among others.[23] As scientists have discovered more theropods closely related to birds, the previously clear distinction between non-birds and birds has become blurred. By the 2000s, discoveries in the [Liaoning](/wiki/Liaoning "Liaoning") Province of northeast China, which demonstrated many small theropod [feathered dinosaurs](/wiki/Feathered_dinosaur "Feathered dinosaur"), contributed to this ambiguity.[24][25][26]

[](/wiki/File:Anchiornis_feathers.jpg)_[Anchiornis huxleyi](/wiki/Anchiornis_huxleyi "Anchiornis huxleyi")_ is an important source of information on the early evolution of birds in the [Late Jurassic](/wiki/Late_Jurassic "Late Jurassic") period.[27]

The consensus view in contemporary [palaeontology](/wiki/Paleontology "Paleontology") is that the flying theropods, or [avialans](/wiki/Avialae "Avialae"), are the closest relatives of the [deinonychosaurs](/wiki/Deinonychosaur "Deinonychosaur"), which include dromaeosaurids and [troodontids](/wiki/Troodontid "Troodontid").[28] Together, these form a group called [Paraves](/wiki/Paraves "Paraves"). Some [basal](/wiki/Basal_\(phylogenetics\) "Basal \(phylogenetics\)") members of Deinonychosauria, such as _[Microraptor](/wiki/Microraptor "Microraptor")_ , have features which may have enabled them to glide or fly. The most basal deinonychosaurs were very small. This evidence raises the possibility that the ancestor of all paravians may have been [arboreal](/wiki/Arboreal "Arboreal"), have been able to glide, or both.[29][30] Unlike _Archaeopteryx_ and the non-avialan feathered dinosaurs, who primarily ate meat, studies suggest that the first avialans were [omnivores](/wiki/Omnivore "Omnivore").[31]

The [Late Jurassic](/wiki/Late_Jurassic "Late Jurassic") _Archaeopteryx_ is well known as one of the first [transitional fossils](/wiki/Transitional_fossil "Transitional fossil") to be found, and it provided support for the [theory of evolution](/wiki/Theory_of_evolution "Theory of evolution") in the late 19th century. _Archaeopteryx_ was the first fossil to display both clearly traditional reptilian characteristics—teeth, clawed fingers, and a long, lizard-like tail—as well as wings with flight feathers similar to those of modern birds. It is not considered a direct ancestor of birds, though it is possibly closely related to the true ancestor.[32]

### Early evolution

See also: [List of fossil bird genera](/wiki/List_of_fossil_bird_genera "List of fossil bird genera")

[](/wiki/File:Confuciusornis_male.jpg)_[Confuciusornis sanctus](/wiki/Confuciusornis_sanctus "Confuciusornis sanctus")_ , a Cretaceous bird from China that lived 125 million years ago, is the oldest known bird to have a beak.[33]

Over 40% of key traits found in modern birds evolved during the 60 million year transition from the earliest [bird-line archosaurs](/wiki/Avemetatarsalia "Avemetatarsalia") to the first [maniraptoromorphs](/wiki/Maniraptoromorpha "Maniraptoromorpha"), i.e. the first dinosaurs closer to living birds than to _[Tyrannosaurus rex](/wiki/Tyrannosaurus "Tyrannosaurus")_. The loss of osteoderms otherwise common in archosaurs and acquisition of primitive feathers might have occurred early during this phase.[14][34] After the appearance of Maniraptoromorpha, the next 40 million years marked a continuous reduction of body size and the accumulation of [neotenic](/wiki/Neoteny "Neoteny") (juvenile-like) characteristics. [Hypercarnivory](/wiki/Hypercarnivore "Hypercarnivore") became increasingly less common while braincases enlarged and forelimbs became longer.[14] The [integument](/wiki/Integument "Integument") evolved into complex, [pennaceous feathers](/wiki/Pennaceous_feather "Pennaceous feather").[34]

The oldest known paravian (and probably the earliest avialan) fossils come from the [Tiaojishan Formation](/wiki/Tiaojishan_Formation "Tiaojishan Formation") of China, which has been dated to the late [Jurassic](/wiki/Jurassic "Jurassic") period ([Oxfordian](/wiki/Oxfordian_\(stage\) "Oxfordian \(stage\)") stage), about 160 million years ago. The avialan species from this time period include _[Anchiornis huxleyi](/wiki/Anchiornis_huxleyi "Anchiornis huxleyi")_ , _[Xiaotingia zhengi](/wiki/Xiaotingia_zhengi "Xiaotingia zhengi")_ , and _[Aurornis xui](/wiki/Aurornis_xui "Aurornis xui")_.[13]

The well-known probable early avialan, _Archaeopteryx_ , dates from slightly later Jurassic rocks (about 155 million years old) from [Germany](/wiki/Germany "Germany"). Many of these early avialans shared unusual anatomical features that may be ancestral to modern birds but were later lost during bird evolution. These features include enlarged claws on the second toe which may have been held clear of the ground in life, and long feathers or "hind wings" covering the hind limbs and feet, which may have been used in aerial maneuvering.[35]

Avialans diversified into a wide variety of forms during the [Cretaceous](/wiki/Cretaceous "Cretaceous") period. Many groups retained [primitive characteristics](/wiki/Symplesiomorphy "Symplesiomorphy"), such as clawed wings and teeth, though the latter were lost independently in a number of avialan groups, including modern birds (Aves).[36] Increasingly stiff tails (especially the outermost half) can be seen in the evolution of maniraptoromorphs, and this process culminated in the appearance of the [pygostyle](/wiki/Pygostyle "Pygostyle"), an ossification of fused tail vertebrae.[14] In the late Cretaceous, about 100 million years ago, the ancestors of all modern birds evolved a more open pelvis, allowing them to lay larger eggs compared to body size.[37] Around 95 million years ago, they evolved a better sense of smell.[38]

A third stage of bird evolution starting with [Ornithothoraces](/wiki/Ornithothoraces "Ornithothoraces") (the "bird-chested" avialans) can be associated with the refining of aerodynamics and flight capabilities, and the loss or co-ossification of several skeletal features. Particularly significant are the development of an enlarged, [keeled](/wiki/Keel_\(bird_anatomy\) "Keel \(bird anatomy\)") sternum and the [alula](/wiki/Alula "Alula"), and the loss of grasping hands. [14]

Mesozoic bird phylogeny simplified after Wang et al., 2015's phylogenetic analysis[39]  
[](/wiki/File:Ichthyornis_Clean.png)_[Ichthyornis](/wiki/Ichthyornis "Ichthyornis")_ , which lived 93 million years ago, was the first known prehistoric bird relative preserved with teeth.

The first large, diverse lineage of short-tailed avialans to evolve were the [Enantiornithes](/wiki/Enantiornithes "Enantiornithes"), or "opposite birds", so named because the construction of their shoulder bones was in reverse to that of modern birds. Enantiornithes occupied a wide array of [ecological niches](/wiki/Ecological_niche "Ecological niche"), from sand-probing shorebirds and fish-eaters to tree-dwelling forms and seed-eaters. While they were the dominant group of avialans during the Cretaceous period, Enantiornithes became extinct along with many other dinosaur groups at the end of the [Mesozoic](/wiki/Mesozoic "Mesozoic") era.[36][40]

Many species of the second major avialan lineage to diversify, the [Euornithes](/wiki/Euornithes "Euornithes") (meaning "true birds", because they include the ancestors of modern birds), were semi-aquatic and specialised in eating fish and other small aquatic organisms. Unlike the Enantiornithes, which dominated land-based and arboreal habitats, most early euornithians lacked [perching](/wiki/Perching_bird "Perching bird") adaptations and likely included shorebird-like species, waders, and swimming and diving species.[41]

The latter included the superficially [gull](/wiki/Gull "Gull")-like _[Ichthyornis](/wiki/Ichthyornis "Ichthyornis")_[42] and the [Hesperornithiformes](/wiki/Hesperornithiformes "Hesperornithiformes"), which became so well adapted to hunting fish in marine environments that they lost the ability to fly and became primarily aquatic.[36] The early euornithians also saw the development of many traits associated with modern birds, like strongly keeled breastbones, toothless, beaked portions of their jaws (though most non-avian euornithians retained teeth in other parts of the jaws).[43] Euornithes also included the first avialans to develop true [pygostyle](/wiki/Pygostyle "Pygostyle") and a fully mobile fan of tail feathers,[44] which may have replaced the "hind wing" as the primary mode of aerial maneuverability and braking in flight.[35]

A study on [mosaic evolution](/wiki/Mosaic_evolution "Mosaic evolution") in the avian skull found that the [last common ancestor](/wiki/Most_recent_common_ancestor "Most recent common ancestor") of all Neornithes might have had a beak similar to that of the modern [hook-billed vanga](/wiki/Hook-billed_vanga "Hook-billed vanga") and a skull similar to that of the [Eurasian golden oriole](/wiki/Eurasian_golden_oriole "Eurasian golden oriole"). As both species are small aerial and canopy foraging omnivores, a similar ecological niche was inferred for this hypothetical ancestor.[45]

### Diversification of modern birds

See also: [Sibley–Ahlquist taxonomy of birds](/wiki/Sibley%E2%80%93Ahlquist_taxonomy_of_birds "Sibley–Ahlquist taxonomy of birds") and [dinosaur classification](/wiki/Dinosaur_classification "Dinosaur classification")

| **Aves** |  | [Palaeognathae](/wiki/Palaeognathae "Palaeognathae") |  ([ratites](/wiki/Ratites "Ratites") and [tinamous](/wiki/Tinamous "Tinamous"))   
---|---  
[Neognathae](/wiki/Neognathae "Neognathae") |  | [Galloanserae](/wiki/Galloanserae "Galloanserae") |  ([landfowl](/wiki/Landfowl "Landfowl") and [waterfowl](/wiki/Waterfowl "Waterfowl"))   
---|---  
[Neoaves](/wiki/Neoaves "Neoaves") |  (all other birds including [perching](/wiki/Passerine "Passerine") birds)   
  
Major groups of modern birds based on [Sibley-Ahlquist taxonomy](/wiki/Sibley-Ahlquist_taxonomy "Sibley-Ahlquist taxonomy")  
  
Most studies agree on a [Cretaceous](/wiki/Cretaceous "Cretaceous") age for the most recent common ancestor of modern birds but estimates range from the Early Cretaceous[46][47] to the latest Cretaceous.[48][49] Similarly, there is no agreement on whether most of the early diversification of modern birds occurred in the Cretaceous and associated with breakup of the supercontinent [Gondwana](/wiki/Gondwana "Gondwana") or occurred later and potentially as a consequence of the [Cretaceous–Palaeogene extinction event](/wiki/Cretaceous%E2%80%93Paleogene_extinction_event "Cretaceous–Paleogene extinction event").[50] This disagreement is in part caused by a divergence in the evidence; most molecular dating studies suggests a Cretaceous [evolutionary radiation](/wiki/Evolutionary_radiation "Evolutionary radiation"), while fossil evidence points to a Cenozoic radiation (the so-called 'rocks' versus 'clocks' controversy). 

The discovery in 2005 of _[Vegavis](/wiki/Vegavis "Vegavis")_ from the [Maastrichtian](/wiki/Maastrichtian "Maastrichtian"), the last stage of the [Late Cretaceous](/wiki/Late_Cretaceous "Late Cretaceous"), proved that the diversification of modern birds started before the [Cenozoic](/wiki/Cenozoic "Cenozoic") era.[51] The affinities of an earlier fossil, the possible [galliform](/wiki/Galliformes "Galliformes") _[Austinornis](/wiki/Austinornis "Austinornis") lentus_, dated to about 85 million years ago,[52] are still too controversial to provide a fossil evidence of modern bird diversification. In 2020, _[Asteriornis](/wiki/Asteriornis "Asteriornis")_ from the Maastrichtian was described, it appears to be a close relative of [Galloanserae](/wiki/Galloanserae "Galloanserae"), the earliest diverging lineage within Neognathae.[1]

Attempts to reconcile molecular and fossil evidence using genomic-scale DNA data and comprehensive fossil information have not resolved the controversy.[48][53] However, a 2015 estimate that used a new method for calibrating [molecular clocks](/wiki/Molecular_clocks "Molecular clocks") confirmed that while modern birds originated early in the Late Cretaceous, likely in Western [Gondwana](/wiki/Gondwana "Gondwana"), a pulse of diversification in all major groups occurred around the Cretaceous–Palaeogene extinction event.[4] Modern birds would have expanded from West Gondwana through two routes. One route was an Antarctic interchange in the Paleogene. The other route was probably via Paleocene land bridges between South America and North America, which allowed for the rapid expansion and diversification of Neornithes into the [Holarctic](/wiki/Holarctic "Holarctic") and [Paleotropics](/wiki/Paleotropics "Paleotropics").[4] On the other hand, the occurrence of _Asteriornis_ in the Northern Hemisphere suggest that Neornithes dispersed out of East Gondwana before the Paleocene.[1]

### Classification of bird orders

See also: [List of birds](/wiki/List_of_birds "List of birds")

All modern birds lie within the [crown group](/wiki/Crown_group "Crown group") Aves (alternately Neornithes), which has two subdivisions: the [Palaeognathae](/wiki/Palaeognathae "Palaeognathae"), which includes the flightless [ratites](/wiki/Ratite "Ratite") (such as the [ostriches](/wiki/Ostrich "Ostrich")) and the weak-flying [tinamous](/wiki/Tinamou "Tinamou"), and the extremely diverse [Neognathae](/wiki/Neognathae "Neognathae"), containing all other birds.[54] These two subdivisions have variously been given the [rank](/wiki/Taxonomic_rank "Taxonomic rank") of [superorder](/wiki/Superorder "Superorder"),[55] cohort,[9] or infraclass.[56] The number of known living bird species is around 11,000[57][58] although sources may differ in their precise numbers. 

[Cladogram](/wiki/Cladogram "Cladogram") of modern bird relationships based on Stiller _et al_ (2024).,[59] showing the 44 orders recognised by the IOC.[57]

The classification of birds is a contentious issue. [Sibley](/wiki/Charles_Sibley "Charles Sibley") and [Ahlquist](/wiki/Jon_Ahlquist "Jon Ahlquist")'s _Phylogeny and Classification of Birds_ (1990) is a landmark work on the subject.[60] Most evidence seems to suggest the assignment of orders is accurate,[61] but scientists disagree about the relationships among the orders themselves; evidence from modern bird anatomy, fossils and DNA have all been brought to bear on the problem, but no strong consensus has emerged. Fossil and molecular evidence from the 2010s is providing an increasingly clear picture of the evolution of modern bird orders.[48][53]

### Genomics

See also: [list of sequenced animal genomes § Birds](/wiki/List_of_sequenced_animal_genomes#Birds "List of sequenced animal genomes")

In 2010, the [genome](/wiki/Genome "Genome") had been sequenced for only two birds, the [chicken](/wiki/Chicken "Chicken") and the [zebra finch](/wiki/Zebra_finch "Zebra finch"). As of 2022[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Bird&action=edit), the genomes of 542 species of birds had been completed. At least one genome has been sequenced from every order.[62][63] These include at least one species in about 90% of extant avian families (218 out of 236 families recognised by the _Howard and Moore Checklist_).[64]

Being able to sequence and compare whole genomes gives researchers many types of information, about genes, the DNA that regulates the genes, and their evolutionary history. This has led to reconsideration of some of the classifications that were based solely on the identification of protein-coding genes. Waterbirds such as [pelicans](/wiki/Pelicans "Pelicans") and [flamingos](/wiki/Flamingos "Flamingos"), for example, may have in common specific adaptations suited to their environment that were developed independently.[62][63]

## Distribution

See also: [Lists of birds by region](/wiki/Lists_of_birds_by_region "Lists of birds by region") and [List of birds by population](/wiki/List_of_birds_by_population "List of birds by population")

[](/wiki/File:House_sparrow04.jpg)The range of the [house sparrow](/wiki/House_sparrow "House sparrow") has expanded dramatically due to human activities.[65]

Birds live and breed in most terrestrial habitats and on all seven continents, reaching their southern extreme in the [snow petrel](/wiki/Snow_petrel "Snow petrel")'s breeding colonies up to 440 kilometres (270 mi) inland in [Antarctica](/wiki/Antarctica "Antarctica").[66] The highest bird [diversity](/wiki/Biodiversity "Biodiversity") occurs in tropical regions. It was earlier thought that this high diversity was the result of higher [speciation](/wiki/Speciation "Speciation") rates in the tropics; however studies from the 2000s found higher speciation rates in the high latitudes that were offset by greater [extinction](/wiki/Extinction "Extinction") rates than in the tropics.[67] Many species migrate annually over great distances and across oceans; several families of birds have adapted to life both on the world's oceans and in them, and some [seabird](/wiki/Seabird "Seabird") species come ashore only to breed,[68] while some [penguins](/wiki/Penguin "Penguin") have been recorded diving up to 300 metres (980 ft) deep.[69]

Many bird species have established breeding populations in areas to which they have been [introduced](/wiki/Introduced_species "Introduced species") by humans. Some of these introductions have been deliberate; the [ring-necked pheasant](/wiki/Ring-necked_pheasant "Ring-necked pheasant"), for example, has been introduced around the world as a [game bird](/wiki/Game_bird "Game bird").[70] Others have been accidental, such as the establishment of wild [monk parakeets](/wiki/Monk_parakeet "Monk parakeet") in several North American cities after their escape from captivity.[71] Some species, including [cattle egret](/wiki/Cattle_egret "Cattle egret"),[72] [yellow-headed caracara](/wiki/Yellow-headed_caracara "Yellow-headed caracara")[73] and [galah](/wiki/Galah "Galah"),[74] have [spread naturally](/wiki/Avian_range_expansion "Avian range expansion") far beyond their original ranges as [agricultural expansion](/wiki/Agriculture "Agriculture") created alternative habitats although modern practices of intensive agriculture have negatively impacted farmland bird populations.[75]

## Anatomy and physiology

Main article: [Bird anatomy](/wiki/Bird_anatomy "Bird anatomy")

See also: [Egg tooth](/wiki/Egg_tooth "Egg tooth")

  2. Head
  3. Iris
  4. Pupil
  5. Mantle
  6. Lesser [coverts](/wiki/Covert_\(feather\) "Covert \(feather\)")
  7. Scapulars
  8. Median coverts
  9. Tertials
  10. Rump
  11. Primaries
