  16. Foot
  17. Tibia
  18. Belly
  19. Flanks
  20. Breast
  21. Throat
  22. Wattle
  23. Eyestripe



Compared with other vertebrates, birds have a [body plan](/wiki/Body_plan "Body plan") that shows many unusual adaptations, mostly to facilitate [flight](/wiki/Bird_flight "Bird flight"). 

### Skeletal system

Main article: [Bird anatomy § Skeletal system](/wiki/Bird_anatomy#Skeletal_system "Bird anatomy")

The skeleton consists of very lightweight bones. They have large air-filled cavities (called pneumatic cavities) which connect with the [respiratory system](/wiki/Respiratory_system "Respiratory system").[76] The skull bones in adults are fused and do not show [cranial sutures](/wiki/Cranial_sutures "Cranial sutures").[77] The [orbital cavities](/wiki/Orbit_\(anatomy\) "Orbit \(anatomy\)") that house the eyeballs are large and separated from each other by a bony [septum](/wiki/Septum "Septum") (partition). The [spine](/wiki/Vertebral_column "Vertebral column") has cervical, thoracic, lumbar and caudal regions with the number of cervical (neck) vertebrae highly variable and especially flexible, but movement is reduced in the anterior [thoracic vertebrae](/wiki/Thoracic_vertebrae "Thoracic vertebrae") and absent in the later vertebrae.[78] The last few are fused with the [pelvis](/wiki/Pelvis "Pelvis") to form the [synsacrum](/wiki/Synsacrum "Synsacrum").[77] The ribs are flattened and the [sternum](/wiki/Sternum "Sternum") is keeled for the attachment of flight muscles except in the flightless bird orders. The forelimbs are modified into wings.[79] The wings are more or less developed depending on the species; the only known groups that lost their wings are the [extinct](/wiki/Extinct "Extinct") [moa](/wiki/Moa "Moa") and [elephant birds](/wiki/Elephant_bird "Elephant bird").[80]

### Excretory system

Like [reptiles](/wiki/Reptile "Reptile"), birds are primarily [uricotelic](/wiki/Metabolic_waste#Uricotelism "Metabolic waste"); that is, their [kidneys](/wiki/Kidney "Kidney") extract [nitrogenous waste](/wiki/Nitrogenous_waste "Nitrogenous waste") from their bloodstream and excrete it as [uric acid](/wiki/Uric_acid "Uric acid"), instead of [urea](/wiki/Urea "Urea") or [ammonia](/wiki/Ammonia "Ammonia"), through the ureters into the intestine. Birds do not have a [urinary bladder](/wiki/Urinary_bladder "Urinary bladder") or external urethral opening. With the exception of the [ostrich](/wiki/Ostrich#Description "Ostrich"), uric acid is excreted along with faeces as a semisolid waste.[81][82][83] However, birds such as hummingbirds can be facultatively ammonotelic, excreting most of the nitrogenous wastes as ammonia.[84] They also excrete [creatine](/wiki/Creatine "Creatine"), rather than [creatinine](/wiki/Creatinine "Creatinine") like mammals.[77] This material, as well as the output of the intestines, emerges from the bird's [cloaca](/wiki/Cloaca "Cloaca").[85][86] The cloaca is a multi-purpose opening: waste is expelled through it, most birds mate by [joining cloaca](/wiki/Bird_anatomy#Reproduction "Bird anatomy"), and females lay eggs from it. In addition, many species of birds regurgitate [pellets](/wiki/Pellet_\(ornithology\) "Pellet \(ornithology\)").[87]

It is a common but not universal feature of [altricial](/wiki/Altriciality "Altriciality") [passerine](/wiki/Passerine "Passerine") nestlings (born helpless, under constant parental care) that instead of excreting directly into the nest, they produce a [fecal sac](/wiki/Fecal_sac "Fecal sac"). This is a mucus-covered pouch that allows parents to either dispose of the waste outside the nest or to recycle the waste through their own digestive system.[88]

[](/wiki/File:20240604_eastern_phoebe_and_fledglings_south_meadows_PD209113.jpg)Adult [eastern phoebe](/wiki/Eastern_phoebe "Eastern phoebe") removing [fecal sac](/wiki/Fecal_sac "Fecal sac") of a fledgling

### Reproductive system

Most male birds do not have [intromittent](/wiki/Intromittent_organ "Intromittent organ") penises.[89] Males within [Palaeognathae](/wiki/Palaeognathae "Palaeognathae") (with the exception of the [kiwis](/wiki/Kiwi_\(bird\) "Kiwi \(bird\)")), the [Anseriformes](/wiki/Anseriformes "Anseriformes") (with the exception of [screamers](/wiki/Screamer "Screamer")), and in rudimentary forms in [Galliformes](/wiki/Galliformes "Galliformes") (but fully developed in [Cracidae](/wiki/Cracidae "Cracidae")) possess a [penis](/wiki/Bird_penis "Bird penis"), which is never present in [Neoaves](/wiki/Neoaves "Neoaves").[90][91] Its length is thought to be related to [sperm competition](/wiki/Sperm_competition "Sperm competition")[92] and it fills with lymphatic fluid instead of blood when erect.[93] When not copulating, it is hidden within the [proctodeum](/wiki/Proctodeum "Proctodeum") compartment within the cloaca, just inside the vent. Female birds have [sperm storage](/wiki/Female_sperm_storage "Female sperm storage") tubules[94] that allow sperm to remain viable long after copulation, a hundred days in some species.[95] Sperm from multiple males may [compete](/wiki/Sperm_competition "Sperm competition") through this mechanism. Most female birds have a single [ovary](/wiki/Ovary "Ovary") and a single [oviduct](/wiki/Oviduct "Oviduct"), both on the left side,[96] but there are exceptions: species in at least 16 different orders of birds have two ovaries. Even these species, however, tend to have a single oviduct.[96] It has been speculated that this might be an adaptation to flight, but males have two testes, and it is also observed that the gonads in both sexes decrease dramatically in size outside the breeding season.[97][98] Also, terrestrial birds generally have a single ovary, as does the [platypus](/wiki/Platypus "Platypus"), an egg-laying mammal. A more likely explanation is that the egg develops a shell while passing through the oviduct over a period of about a day, so that if two eggs were to develop at the same time, there would be a risk to survival.[96] While rare, mostly abortive, [parthenogenesis](/wiki/Parthenogenesis "Parthenogenesis") is not unknown in birds and eggs can be [diploid](/wiki/Ploidy#Diploid "Ploidy"), [automictic](/wiki/Automixis "Automixis") and results in male offspring.[99]

Birds are solely [gonochoric](/wiki/Gonochorism "Gonochorism"),[100] meaning they have two sexes: either [female](/wiki/Female "Female") or [male](/wiki/Male "Male"). The sex of birds follows the [Z and W sex-determination system](/wiki/ZW_sex-determination_system "ZW sex-determination system"), where the [ovum](/wiki/Egg_cell "Egg cell") determines the sex of the offspring. Male birds have two Z chromosomes (ZZ), and female birds have a W chromosome and a Z chromosome (WZ).[77] In [mammals](/wiki/Mammal "Mammal"), by contrast, the [sperm](/wiki/Sperm "Sperm") determines the sex of the offspring in the [X and Y sex-determination](/wiki/XY_sex-determination_system "XY sex-determination system") system. A complex system of [disassortative mating](/wiki/Disassortative_mating "Disassortative mating") with two morphs is involved in the [white-throated sparrow](/wiki/White-throated_sparrow "White-throated sparrow") _Zonotrichia albicollis_ , where white- and tan-browed morphs of opposite sex pair, making it appear as if four sexes were involved since any individual is compatible with only a fourth of the population.[101] In all species of birds, an individual's sex is determined at fertilisation. One 2007 study claimed to demonstrate [temperature-dependent sex determination](/wiki/Temperature-dependent_sex_determination "Temperature-dependent sex determination") among the [Australian brushturkey](/wiki/Australian_brushturkey "Australian brushturkey"), for which higher temperatures during incubation resulted in a higher female-to-male [sex ratio](/wiki/Sex_ratio "Sex ratio").[102] This, however, was later proven to not be the case. These birds do not exhibit temperature-dependent sex determination, but temperature-dependent sex mortality.[103]

### Respiratory and circulatory systems

Birds have one of the most complex [respiratory systems](/wiki/Respiratory_system "Respiratory system") of all animal groups.[77] Upon inhalation, 75% of the fresh air bypasses the lungs and flows directly into a posterior [air sac](/wiki/Parabronchi "Parabronchi") which extends from the lungs and connects with air spaces in the bones and fills them with air. The other 25% of the air goes directly into the lungs. When the bird exhales, the used air flows out of the lungs and the stored fresh air from the posterior air sac is simultaneously forced into the lungs. Thus, a bird's lungs receive a constant supply of fresh air during both inhalation and exhalation.[104] Sound production is achieved using the [syrinx](/wiki/Syrinx_\(biology\) "Syrinx \(biology\)"), a muscular chamber incorporating multiple tympanic membranes which diverges from the lower end of the trachea;[105] the trachea being elongated in some species, increasing the volume of vocalisations and the perception of the bird's size.[106]

In birds, the main arteries taking blood away from the heart originate from the right [aortic arch](/wiki/Aortic_arches "Aortic arches") (or pharyngeal arch), unlike in the mammals where the left aortic arch forms this part of the [aorta](/wiki/Aorta "Aorta").[77] The postcava receives blood from the limbs via the renal portal system. Unlike in mammals, the circulating [red blood cells](/wiki/Red_blood_cells "Red blood cells") in birds retain their [nucleus](/wiki/Cell_nucleus "Cell nucleus").[107]

#### Heart type and features

[](/wiki/File:Didactic_model_of_an_avian_heart-FMVZ_USP-13_\(cropped\).jpg)[Didactic model](/wiki/Educational_toy "Educational toy") of an avian heart

The avian circulatory system is driven by a four-chambered, myogenic heart contained in a fibrous pericardial sac. This pericardial sac is filled with a [serous fluid](/wiki/Serous_fluid "Serous fluid") for lubrication.[108] The heart itself is divided into a right and left half, each with an [atrium](/wiki/Atrium_\(heart\) "Atrium \(heart\)") and [ventricle](/wiki/Ventricle_\(heart\) "Ventricle \(heart\)"). The atrium and ventricles of each side are separated by [atrioventricular valves](/wiki/Atrioventricular_valves "Atrioventricular valves") which prevent back flow from one chamber to the next during contraction. Being myogenic, the heart's pace is maintained by pacemaker cells found in the sinoatrial node, located on the right atrium.[109]

The [sinoatrial node](/wiki/Sinoatrial_node "Sinoatrial node") uses calcium to cause a [depolarising](/wiki/Depolarization "Depolarization") [signal transduction pathway](/wiki/Signal_transduction_pathway "Signal transduction pathway") from the atrium through right and left atrioventricular bundle which communicates contraction to the ventricles. The avian heart also consists of muscular arches that are made up of thick bundles of muscular layers. Much like a mammalian heart, the avian heart is composed of [endocardial](/wiki/Endocardial "Endocardial"), [myocardial](/wiki/Myocardial "Myocardial") and [epicardial](/wiki/Epicardial "Epicardial") layers.[108] The atrium walls tend to be thinner than the ventricle walls, due to the intense ventricular contraction used to pump oxygenated blood throughout the body. Avian hearts are generally larger than mammalian hearts when compared to body mass. This adaptation allows more blood to be pumped to meet the high metabolic need associated with flight.[110]

#### Organisation

Birds have a very efficient system for diffusing oxygen into the blood; birds have a ten times greater surface area to [gas exchange](/wiki/Gas_exchange "Gas exchange") volume than mammals. As a result, birds have more blood in their capillaries per unit of volume of lung than a mammal.[110] The arteries are composed of thick elastic muscles to withstand the pressure of the ventricular contractions, and become more rigid as they move away from the heart. Blood moves through the arteries, which undergo [vasoconstriction](/wiki/Vasoconstriction "Vasoconstriction"), and into arterioles which act as a transportation system to distribute primarily oxygen as well as nutrients to all tissues of the body. As the arterioles move away from the heart and into individual organs and tissues they are further divided to increase surface area and slow blood flow. Blood travels through the arterioles and moves into the capillaries where gas exchange can occur.[111]

Capillaries are organised into capillary beds in tissues; it is here that blood exchanges oxygen for carbon dioxide waste. In the capillary beds, blood flow is slowed to allow maximum [diffusion](/wiki/Diffusion "Diffusion") of oxygen into the tissues. Once the blood has become deoxygenated, it travels through venules then veins and back to the heart. Veins, unlike arteries, are thin and rigid as they do not need to withstand extreme pressure. As blood travels through the venules to the veins a funneling occurs called [vasodilation](/wiki/Vasodilation "Vasodilation") bringing blood back to the heart.[111] Once the blood reaches the heart, it moves first into the right atrium, then the right ventricle to be pumped through the lungs for further gas exchange of carbon dioxide waste for oxygen. Oxygenated blood then flows from the lungs through the left atrium to the left ventricle where it is pumped out to the body.[19]

### Nervous system

Main article: [Bird anatomy § Nervous system](/wiki/Bird_anatomy#Nervous_system "Bird anatomy")

The [nervous system](/wiki/Nervous_system "Nervous system") is large relative to the bird's size.[77] The most developed part of the [brain of birds](/wiki/Avian_brain "Avian brain") is the one that controls the flight-related functions, while the [cerebellum](/wiki/Cerebellum "Cerebellum") coordinates movement and the [cerebrum](/wiki/Cerebrum "Cerebrum") controls behaviour patterns, navigation, mating and [nest](/wiki/Bird_nest "Bird nest") building. Most birds have a poor [sense of smell](/wiki/Olfaction "Olfaction")[112] with notable exceptions including [kiwis](/wiki/Kiwi_\(bird\) "Kiwi \(bird\)"),[113] [New World vultures](/wiki/New_World_vulture "New World vulture")[114] and [tubenoses](/wiki/Procellariiformes "Procellariiformes").[115] The [avian visual system](/wiki/Bird_vision "Bird vision") is usually highly developed. Water birds have special flexible lenses, allowing [accommodation](/wiki/Accommodation_\(eye\) "Accommodation \(eye\)") for vision in air and water.[77] Some species also have dual [fovea](/wiki/Fovea_centralis "Fovea centralis"). Birds are [tetrachromatic](/wiki/Tetrachromacy "Tetrachromacy"), possessing [ultraviolet](/wiki/Ultraviolet "Ultraviolet") (UV) sensitive [cone cells](/wiki/Cone_cell "Cone cell") in the eye as well as green, red and blue ones.[116] They also have [double cones](/wiki/Double_cone_\(biology\) "Double cone \(biology\)"), likely to mediate [achromatic vision](/wiki/Monochromacy "Monochromacy").[117]

[](/wiki/File:Bird_blink-edit.jpg)The [nictitating membrane](/wiki/Nictitating_membrane "Nictitating membrane") as it covers the eye of a [masked lapwing](/wiki/Masked_lapwing "Masked lapwing")

Many birds show plumage patterns in [ultraviolet](/wiki/Ultraviolet "Ultraviolet") that are invisible to the human eye; some birds whose sexes appear similar to the naked eye are distinguished by the presence of ultraviolet reflective patches on their feathers. Male [blue tits](/wiki/Blue_tit "Blue tit") have an ultraviolet reflective crown patch which is displayed in courtship by posturing and raising of their nape feathers.[118] Ultraviolet light is also used in foraging—[kestrels](/wiki/Kestrel "Kestrel") have been shown to search for prey by detecting the UV fluorescent urine trail marks left on the ground by rodents.[119] With the exception of pigeons and a few other species,[120] the eyelids of birds are not used in blinking. Instead the eye is lubricated by the [nictitating membrane](/wiki/Nictitating_membrane "Nictitating membrane"), a third eyelid that moves horizontally.[121] The nictitating membrane also covers the eye and acts as a [contact lens](/wiki/Contact_lens "Contact lens") in many aquatic birds.[77] The bird [retina](/wiki/Retina "Retina") has a fan shaped blood supply system called the [pecten](/wiki/Pecten_oculi "Pecten oculi").[77]

[Eyes](/wiki/Eye "Eye") of most birds are large, not very round and capable of only limited movement in the orbits,[77] typically 10–20°.[122] Birds with eyes on the sides of their heads have a wide [visual field](/wiki/Visual_field "Visual field"), while birds with eyes on the front of their heads, such as owls, have [binocular vision](/wiki/Binocular_vision "Binocular vision") and can estimate the [depth of field](/wiki/Depth_of_field "Depth of field").[122][123] The avian [ear](/wiki/Ear "Ear") lacks external [pinnae](/wiki/Pinna_\(anatomy\) "Pinna \(anatomy\)") but is covered by feathers, although in some birds, such as the _[Asio](/wiki/Asio "Asio")_ , _[Bubo](/wiki/Horned_owl "Horned owl")_ and _[Otus](/wiki/Scops_owl "Scops owl")_ [owls](/wiki/Owl "Owl"), these feathers form tufts which resemble ears. The [inner ear](/wiki/Inner_ear "Inner ear") has a [cochlea](/wiki/Cochlea "Cochlea"), but it is not a spiral as in mammals.[124] Several species have been demonstrated to hear infrasound (below 20 Hz)[125] and a few cave-dwelling swifts and oilbirds emit ultrasound (above 20 kHz) and [echolocate](/wiki/Animal_echolocation "Animal echolocation") in darkness.[126]

### Defence and intraspecific combat

A few species are able to use chemical defences against predators; some [Procellariiformes](/wiki/Procellariiformes "Procellariiformes") can eject an unpleasant [stomach oil](/wiki/Stomach_oil "Stomach oil") against an aggressor,[127] and some species of [pitohuis](/wiki/Pitohui "Pitohui") from [New Guinea](/wiki/New_Guinea "New Guinea") have a powerful [neurotoxin](/wiki/Neurotoxin "Neurotoxin") in their skin and feathers.[128]

A lack of field observations limit our knowledge, but intraspecific conflicts are known to sometimes result in injury or death.[129] The screamers ([Anhimidae](/wiki/Anhimidae "Anhimidae")), some jacanas (_[Jacana](/wiki/Jacana_\(genus\) "Jacana \(genus\)")_ , _[Hydrophasianus](/wiki/Hydrophasianus "Hydrophasianus")_), the spur-winged goose (_[Plectropterus](/wiki/Plectropterus "Plectropterus")_), the torrent duck (_[Merganetta](/wiki/Merganetta "Merganetta")_) and nine species of lapwing (_[Vanellus](/wiki/Vanellus "Vanellus")_) use a sharp spur on the wing as a weapon. The steamer ducks (_[Tachyeres](/wiki/Tachyeres "Tachyeres")_), geese and swans (_[Anserinae](/wiki/Anserinae "Anserinae")_), the solitaire (_[Pezophaps](/wiki/Pezophaps "Pezophaps")_), sheathbills (_[Chionis](/wiki/Chionis "Chionis")_), some guans (_[Crax](/wiki/Crax "Crax")_) and stone curlews (_[Burhinus](/wiki/Burhinus "Burhinus")_) use a bony knob on the [alular](/wiki/Alula "Alula") metacarpal to punch and hammer opponents.[129] The jacanas _[Actophilornis](/wiki/Actophilornis "Actophilornis")_ and _[Irediparra](/wiki/Irediparra "Irediparra")_ have an expanded, blade-like radius. The extinct _[Xenicibis](/wiki/Xenicibis "Xenicibis")_ was unique in having an elongate forelimb and massive hand which likely functioned in combat or defence as a jointed club or flail. [Swans](/wiki/Cygnus_olor "Cygnus olor"), for instance, may strike with the bony spurs and bite when defending eggs or young.[129]

### Feathers, plumage, and scales

Main articles: [Feather](/wiki/Feather "Feather"), [Flight feather](/wiki/Flight_feather "Flight feather"), and [Down feather](/wiki/Down_feather "Down feather")

[](/wiki/File:African_Scops_owl.jpg)The [disruptively patterned](/wiki/Disruptively_patterned "Disruptively patterned") plumage of the [African scops owl](/wiki/African_scops_owl "African scops owl") allows it to blend in with its surroundings.

Feathers are a feature characteristic of birds (though also present in [some dinosaurs](/wiki/Feathered_dinosaurs "Feathered dinosaurs") not currently considered to be true birds). They facilitate [flight](/wiki/Bird_flight "Bird flight"), provide insulation that aids in [thermoregulation](/wiki/Thermoregulation "Thermoregulation"), and are used in display, camouflage, and signalling.[77] There are several types of feathers, each serving its own set of purposes. Feathers are epidermal growths attached to the skin and arise only in specific tracts of skin called [pterylae](/wiki/Pterylography "Pterylography"). The distribution pattern of these feather tracts (pterylosis) is used in taxonomy and systematics. The arrangement and appearance of feathers on the body, called [plumage](/wiki/Plumage "Plumage"), may vary within species by age, [social status](/wiki/Social_status "Social status"),[130] and [sex](/wiki/Sexual_dimorphism "Sexual dimorphism").[131]

Plumage is regularly [moulted](/wiki/Moult "Moult"); the standard plumage of a bird that has moulted after breeding is known as the "[non-breeding](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#basic_plumage "Glossary of bird terms")" plumage, or—in the [Humphrey–Parkes terminology](/wiki/Humphrey%E2%80%93Parkes_terminology "Humphrey–Parkes terminology")—"basic" plumage; breeding plumages or variations of the basic plumage are known under the Humphrey–Parkes system as "[alternate](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#alternate_plumage "Glossary of bird terms")" plumages.[132] Moulting is annual in most species, although some may have two moults a year, and large birds of prey may moult only once every few years. Moulting patterns vary across species. In passerines, [flight feathers](/wiki/Flight_feather "Flight feather") are replaced one at a time with the innermost [primary](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#primary "Glossary of bird terms") being the first. When the fifth of sixth primary is replaced, the outermost [tertiaries](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#tertiaries "Glossary of bird terms") begin to drop. After the innermost tertiaries are moulted, the [secondaries](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#secondaries "Glossary of bird terms") starting from the innermost begin to drop and this proceeds to the outer feathers (centrifugal moult). The greater primary [coverts](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#coverts "Glossary of bird terms") are moulted in synchrony with the primary that they overlap.[133]

[](/wiki/File:Red_Lory_\(Eos_bornea\)-6.jpg)[Red lory](/wiki/Red_lory "Red lory") preening

A small number of species, such as ducks and geese, lose all of their flight feathers at once, temporarily becoming flightless.[134] As a general rule, the tail feathers are moulted and replaced starting with the innermost pair.[133] Centripetal moults of tail feathers are however seen in the [Phasianidae](/wiki/Phasianidae "Phasianidae").[135] The centrifugal moult is modified in the tail feathers of [woodpeckers](/wiki/Woodpecker "Woodpecker") and [treecreepers](/wiki/Treecreeper "Treecreeper"), in that it begins with the second innermost pair of feathers and finishes with the central pair of feathers so that the bird maintains a functional climbing tail.[133][136] The general pattern seen in [passerines](/wiki/Passerine "Passerine") is that the primaries are replaced outward, secondaries inward, and the tail from centre outward.[137] Before nesting, the females of most bird species gain a bare [brood patch](/wiki/Brood_patch "Brood patch") by losing feathers close to the belly. The skin there is well supplied with blood vessels and helps the bird in incubation.[138]

Feathers require maintenance and birds [preen](/wiki/Preening "Preening") or groom them daily, spending an average of around 9% of their daily time on this.[139] The bill is used to brush away foreign particles and to apply [waxy](/wiki/Wax "Wax") secretions from the [uropygial gland](/wiki/Uropygial_gland "Uropygial gland"); these secretions protect the feathers' flexibility and act as an [antimicrobial agent](/wiki/Antimicrobial "Antimicrobial"), inhibiting the growth of feather-degrading [bacteria](/wiki/Bacteria "Bacteria").[140] This may be supplemented with the secretions of [formic acid](/wiki/Formic_acid "Formic acid") from ants, which birds receive through a behaviour known as [anting](/wiki/Anting_\(bird_activity\) "Anting \(bird activity\)"), to remove feather parasites.[141]

The [scales](/wiki/Bird_anatomy#Scales "Bird anatomy") of birds are composed of the same keratin as beaks, claws, and spurs. They are found mainly on the toes and [metatarsus](/wiki/Metatarsus "Metatarsus"), but may be found further up on the ankle in some birds. Most bird scales do not overlap significantly, except in the cases of [kingfishers](/wiki/Kingfisher "Kingfisher") and [woodpeckers](/wiki/Woodpecker "Woodpecker"). The scales of birds are thought to be [homologous](/wiki/Homology_\(biology\) "Homology \(biology\)") to those of reptiles and mammals.[142]

### Flight

Main articles: [Bird flight](/wiki/Bird_flight "Bird flight") and [Flightless birds](/wiki/Flightless_birds "Flightless birds")

[](/wiki/File:Restless_flycatcher04.jpg)[Restless flycatcher](/wiki/Restless_flycatcher "Restless flycatcher") in the downstroke of flapping flight

Most birds can [fly](/wiki/Flying_and_gliding_animals "Flying and gliding animals"), which distinguishes them from almost all other vertebrate classes. Flight is the primary means of locomotion for most bird species and is used for searching for food and for escaping from predators. Birds have various adaptations for flight, including a lightweight skeleton, two large flight muscles, the pectoralis (which accounts for 15% of the total mass of the bird) and the supracoracoideus, as well as a modified forelimb ([wing](/wiki/Bird_wing "Bird wing")) that serves as an [aerofoil](/wiki/Airfoil "Airfoil").[77]

Wing shape and size generally determine a bird's flight style and performance; many birds combine powered, flapping flight with less energy-intensive soaring flight. About 60 extant bird species are [flightless](/wiki/Flightless_bird "Flightless bird"), as were many extinct birds.[143] Flightlessness often arises in birds on isolated islands, most likely due to limited resources and the absence of [mammalian](/wiki/Mammal "Mammal") land predators.[144] Flightlessness is almost exclusively correlated with [gigantism](/wiki/Island_gigantism "Island gigantism") due to an island's inherent condition of isolation.[145][146] Although flightless, penguins use similar musculature and movements to "fly" through the water, as do some flight-capable birds such as [auks](/wiki/Auk "Auk"), [shearwaters](/wiki/Shearwater "Shearwater") and [dippers](/wiki/Dipper "Dipper").[147]

## Behaviour

Most birds are [diurnal](/wiki/Diurnal_animal "Diurnal animal"), but some birds, such as many species of [owls](/wiki/Owl "Owl") and [nightjars](/wiki/Nightjar "Nightjar"), are [nocturnal](/wiki/Nocturnal "Nocturnal") or [crepuscular](/wiki/Crepuscular "Crepuscular") (active during twilight hours), and many coastal [waders](/wiki/Wader "Wader") feed when the tides are appropriate, by day or night.[148]

### Diet and feeding

See also: [Bird food](/wiki/Bird_food "Bird food")

[](/wiki/File:BirdBeaksA.svg)Feeding adaptations in beaks [](/wiki/File:Paloma_\(3771094735\).jpg)[Feral pigeon](/wiki/Feral_pigeon "Feral pigeon") eating bread.

[Birds' diets](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#dietary_classification_terms_\(-vores\) "Glossary of bird terms") are varied and often include [nectar](/wiki/Nectar_\(plant\) "Nectar \(plant\)"), fruit, plants, seeds, [carrion](/wiki/Carrion "Carrion"), and various small animals, including other birds.[77] The [digestive system of birds](/wiki/Digestive_system_of_birds "Digestive system of birds") is unique, with a [crop](/wiki/Crop_\(anatomy\) "Crop \(anatomy\)") for storage and a [gizzard](/wiki/Gizzard "Gizzard") that contains swallowed stones for grinding food to compensate for the lack of teeth.[149] Some species such as pigeons and some psittacine species do not have a [gallbladder](/wiki/Gallbladder "Gallbladder").[150] Most birds are highly adapted for rapid digestion to aid with flight.[151] Some migratory birds have adapted to use protein stored in many parts of their bodies, including protein from the intestines, as additional energy during migration.[152]

Birds that employ many strategies to obtain food or feed on a variety of food items are called generalists, while others that concentrate time and effort on specific food items or have a single strategy to obtain food are considered specialists.[77] [Avian foraging](/wiki/Avian_foraging "Avian foraging") strategies can vary widely by species. Many birds [glean](/wiki/Gleaning_\(birds\) "Gleaning \(birds\)") for insects, invertebrates, fruit, or seeds. Some hunt insects by suddenly attacking from a branch. Those species that seek [pest](/wiki/Pest_\(organism\) "Pest \(organism\)") [insects](/wiki/Insect "Insect") are considered beneficial 'biological control agents' and their presence encouraged in [biological pest control](/wiki/Biological_pest_control "Biological pest control") programmes.[153] Combined, insectivorous birds eat 400–500 million metric tons of arthropods annually.[154]

Nectar feeders such as [hummingbirds](/wiki/Hummingbird "Hummingbird"), [sunbirds](/wiki/Sunbird "Sunbird"), [lories, and lorikeets](/wiki/Lories_and_lorikeets "Lories and lorikeets") amongst others have specially adapted brushy tongues and in many cases bills designed to fit [co-adapted](/wiki/Coevolution "Coevolution") flowers.[155] [Kiwis](/wiki/Kiwi_\(bird\) "Kiwi \(bird\)") and [shorebirds](/wiki/Shorebird "Shorebird") with long bills probe for invertebrates; shorebirds' varied bill lengths and feeding methods result in the separation of [ecological niches](/wiki/Ecological_niche "Ecological niche").[77][156] [Divers](/wiki/Loon "Loon"), [diving ducks](/wiki/Diving_duck "Diving duck"), [penguins](/wiki/Penguin "Penguin") and [auks](/wiki/Auks "Auks") pursue their prey underwater, using their wings or feet for propulsion,[68] while aerial predators such as [sulids](/wiki/Sulidae "Sulidae"), [kingfishers](/wiki/Kingfisher "Kingfisher") and [terns](/wiki/Tern "Tern") plunge dive after their prey. [Flamingos](/wiki/Flamingo "Flamingo"), three species of [prion](/wiki/Prion_\(bird\) "Prion \(bird\)"), and some ducks are [filter feeders](/wiki/Filter_feeder "Filter feeder").[157][158] [Geese](/wiki/Geese "Geese") and [dabbling ducks](/wiki/Dabbling_duck "Dabbling duck") are primarily grazers.[159][160]

Some species, including [frigatebirds](/wiki/Frigatebird "Frigatebird"), [gulls](/wiki/Gull "Gull"),[161] and [skuas](/wiki/Skua "Skua"),[162] engage in [kleptoparasitism](/wiki/Kleptoparasitism "Kleptoparasitism"), stealing food items from other birds. Kleptoparasitism is thought to be a supplement to food obtained by hunting, rather than a significant part of any species' diet; a study of [great frigatebirds](/wiki/Great_frigatebird "Great frigatebird") stealing from [masked boobies](/wiki/Masked_booby "Masked booby") estimated that the frigatebirds stole at most 40% of their food and on average stole only 5%.[163] Other birds are [scavengers](/wiki/Scavenger "Scavenger"); some of these, like [vultures](/wiki/Vulture "Vulture"), are specialised carrion eaters, while others, like gulls, [corvids](/wiki/Corvid "Corvid"), or other birds of prey, are opportunists.[164]

### Water and drinking

Water is needed by many birds although their mode of excretion and lack of [sweat glands](/wiki/Sweat_gland "Sweat gland") reduces the physiological demands.[165] Some desert birds can obtain their water needs entirely from moisture in their food. Some have other adaptations such as allowing their body temperature to rise, saving on moisture loss from evaporative cooling or panting.[166] Seabirds can drink seawater and have [salt glands](/wiki/Salt_gland "Salt gland") inside the head that eliminate excess salt out of the nostrils.[167]

Most birds scoop water in their beaks and raise their head to let water run down the throat. Some species, especially of arid zones, belonging to the [pigeon](/wiki/Columbidae "Columbidae"), [finch](/wiki/Estrildidae "Estrildidae"), [mousebird](/wiki/Coliidae "Coliidae"), [button-quail](/wiki/Turnicidae "Turnicidae") and [bustard](/wiki/Otididae "Otididae") families are capable of sucking up water without the need to tilt back their heads.[168] Some desert birds depend on water sources and [sandgrouse](/wiki/Sandgrouse "Sandgrouse") are particularly well known for congregating daily at waterholes. Nesting sandgrouse and many plovers carry water to their young by wetting their belly feathers.[169] Some birds carry water for chicks at the nest in their crop or regurgitate it along with food. The pigeon family, flamingos and penguins have adaptations to produce a nutritive fluid called [crop milk](/wiki/Crop_milk "Crop milk") that they provide to their chicks.[170]

### Migration

Main article: [Bird migration](/wiki/Bird_migration "Bird migration")

[](/wiki/File:CanadianGeeseFlyingInVFormation.jpg)A flock of [Canada geese](/wiki/Canada_geese "Canada geese") in [V formation](/wiki/V_formation "V formation")

Many bird species migrate to take advantage of global differences of [seasonal](/wiki/Season "Season") temperatures, therefore optimising availability of food sources and breeding habitat. These migrations vary among the different groups. Many landbirds, [shorebirds](/wiki/Shorebird "Shorebird"), and [waterbirds](/wiki/Waterbird "Waterbird") undertake annual long-distance migrations, usually triggered by the length of daylight as well as weather conditions. These birds are characterised by a breeding season spent in the [temperate](/wiki/Temperate "Temperate") or [polar regions](/wiki/Polar_region "Polar region") and a non-breeding season in the [tropical](/wiki/Tropical "Tropical") regions or opposite hemisphere. Before migration, birds substantially increase body fats and reserves and reduce the size of some of their organs.[171][172]

Migration is highly demanding energetically, particularly as birds need to cross deserts and oceans without refuelling. Landbirds have a flight range of around 2,500 km (1,600 mi) and shorebirds can fly up to 4,000 km (2,500 mi),[77] although the [bar-tailed godwit](/wiki/Bar-tailed_godwit "Bar-tailed godwit") is capable of non-stop flights of up to 10,200 km (6,300 mi).[173] Some [seabirds](/wiki/Seabird "Seabird") undertake long migrations, with the longest annual migrations including those of [Arctic terns](/wiki/Arctic_terns "Arctic terns"), which were recorded travelling an average of 70,900 km (44,100 mi) between their [Arctic](/wiki/Arctic "Arctic") breeding grounds in [Greenland](/wiki/Greenland "Greenland") and [Iceland](/wiki/Iceland "Iceland") and their wintering grounds in [Antarctica](/wiki/Antarctica "Antarctica"), with one bird covering 81,600 km (50,700 mi),[174] and [sooty shearwaters](/wiki/Sooty_shearwater "Sooty shearwater"), which nest in [New Zealand](/wiki/New_Zealand "New Zealand") and [Chile](/wiki/Chile "Chile") and make annual round trips of 64,000 km (39,800 mi) to their summer feeding grounds in the North Pacific off Japan, [Alaska](/wiki/Alaska "Alaska") and [California](/wiki/California "California").[175] Other seabirds disperse after breeding, travelling widely but having no set migration route. [Albatrosses](/wiki/Albatross "Albatross") nesting in the [Southern Ocean](/wiki/Southern_Ocean "Southern Ocean") often undertake circumpolar trips between breeding seasons.[176]

[](/wiki/File:Bar-tailed_Godwit_migration.jpg)The routes of satellite-tagged [bar-tailed godwits](/wiki/Bar-tailed_godwit "Bar-tailed godwit") migrating north from [New Zealand](/wiki/New_Zealand "New Zealand"). This species has the longest known non-stop migration of any species, up to 10,200 km (6,300 mi).

Some bird species undertake shorter migrations, travelling only as far as is required to avoid bad weather or obtain food. [Irruptive](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/irruptive "wikt:irruptive") species such as the boreal [finches](/wiki/Finch "Finch") are one such group and can commonly be found at a location in one year and absent the next. This type of migration is normally associated with food availability.[177] Species may also travel shorter distances over part of their range, with individuals from higher latitudes travelling into the existing range of conspecifics; others undertake partial migrations, where only a fraction of the population, usually females and subdominant males, migrates.[178] Partial migration can form a large percentage of the migration behaviour of birds in some regions; in Australia, surveys found that 44% of non-passerine birds and 32% of passerines were partially migratory.[179]

[Altitudinal migration](/wiki/Altitudinal_migration "Altitudinal migration") is a form of short-distance migration in which birds spend the breeding season at higher altitudes and move to lower ones during suboptimal conditions. It is most often triggered by temperature changes and usually occurs when [the normal territories](/wiki/Territory_\(animal\) "Territory \(animal\)") also become inhospitable due to lack of food.[180] Some species may also be nomadic, holding no fixed territory and moving according to weather and food availability. [Parrots](/wiki/True_parrots "True parrots") as a [family](/wiki/Family_\(taxonomy\) "Family \(taxonomy\)") are overwhelmingly neither migratory nor sedentary but considered to either be dispersive, irruptive, nomadic or undertake small and irregular migrations.[181]

The ability of birds to return to precise locations across vast distances has been known for some time; in an experiment conducted in the 1950s, a [Manx shearwater](/wiki/Manx_shearwater "Manx shearwater") released in [Boston](/wiki/Boston "Boston") in the United States returned to its colony in [Skomer](/wiki/Skomer "Skomer"), in Wales within 13 days, a distance of 5,150 km (3,200 mi).[182] Birds navigate during migration using a variety of methods. For [diurnal](/wiki/Diurnal_animal "Diurnal animal") migrants, the [sun](/wiki/Sun "Sun") is used to navigate by day, and a stellar compass is used at night. Birds that use the sun compensate for the changing position of the sun during the day by the use of an [internal clock](/wiki/Chronobiology "Chronobiology").[77] Orientation with the stellar compass depends on the position of the [constellations](/wiki/Constellation "Constellation") surrounding [Polaris](/wiki/Polaris "Polaris").[183] These are backed up in some species by their ability to sense the Earth's [geomagnetism](/wiki/Geomagnetism "Geomagnetism") through specialised [photoreceptors](/wiki/Photoreceptor_cell "Photoreceptor cell").[184]

### Communication

See also: [Bird vocalisation](/wiki/Bird_vocalisation "Bird vocalisation")

[Bird song](/wiki/File:Troglodytes_aedon_-_House_Wren_-_XC79974.ogg "File:Troglodytes aedon - House Wren - XC79974.ogg")

Song of the [house wren](/wiki/Northern_house_wren "Northern house wren"), a common North American songbird

* * *

[Mimicry](/wiki/File:Tooth-billed_Catbird_audio09.ogg "File:Tooth-billed Catbird audio09.ogg")

A [tooth-billed bowerbird](/wiki/Tooth-billed_bowerbird "Tooth-billed bowerbird") mimicking a [spangled drongo](/wiki/Spangled_drongo "Spangled drongo")

* * *

[Drumming](/wiki/File:Picidae_pecking_on_wood.ogg "File:Picidae pecking on wood.ogg")

A [woodpecker](/wiki/Woodpecker "Woodpecker") drumming on wood

* * *

_Problems playing these files? See[media help](/wiki/Help:Media "Help:Media")._

Birds [communicate](/wiki/Animal_communication "Animal communication") primarily using visual and auditory signals. Signals can be interspecific (between species) and intraspecific (within species). 

Birds sometimes use plumage to assess and assert social dominance,[185] to display breeding condition in sexually selected species, or to make threatening displays, as in the [sunbittern](/wiki/Sunbittern "Sunbittern")'s mimicry of a large predator to ward off [hawks](/wiki/Hawk "Hawk") and protect young chicks.[186]

[](/wiki/File:Stavenn_Eurypiga_helias_00.jpg)The startling display of the [sunbittern](/wiki/Sunbittern "Sunbittern") mimics a large predator.

Visual communication among birds may also involve ritualised displays, which have developed from non-signalling actions such as preening, the adjustments of feather position, pecking, or other behaviour. These displays may signal aggression or submission or may contribute to the formation of pair-bonds.[77] The most elaborate displays occur during courtship, where "dances" are often formed from complex combinations of many possible component movements;[187] males' breeding success may depend on the quality of such displays.[188]

[Bird calls and songs](/wiki/Bird_vocalization "Bird vocalization"), which are produced in the [syrinx](/wiki/Syrinx_\(biology\) "Syrinx \(biology\)"), are the major means by which birds communicate with [sound](/wiki/Sound "Sound"). This communication can be very complex; some species can operate the two sides of the syrinx independently, allowing the simultaneous production of two different songs.[105] Calls are used for a variety of purposes, including mate attraction,[77] evaluation of potential mates,[189] bond formation, the claiming and maintenance of territories,[77][190] the identification of other individuals (such as when parents look for chicks in colonies or when mates reunite at the start of breeding season),[191] and the warning of other birds of potential predators, sometimes with specific information about the nature of the threat.[192] Some birds also use mechanical sounds for auditory communication. The _[Coenocorypha](/wiki/Coenocorypha "Coenocorypha")_ [snipes](/wiki/Snipe "Snipe") of [New Zealand](/wiki/New_Zealand "New Zealand") drive air through their feathers,[193] [woodpeckers](/wiki/Woodpecker "Woodpecker") drum for long-distance communication,[194] and [palm cockatoos](/wiki/Palm_cockatoo "Palm cockatoo") use tools to drum.[195]

### Flocking and other associations

[](/wiki/File:Red-billed_quelea_flocking_at_waterhole.jpg)[Red-billed queleas](/wiki/Red-billed_quelea "Red-billed quelea"), the most numerous species of wild bird,[196] form enormous flocks - sometimes tens of thousands strong.

While some birds are essentially territorial or live in small family groups, other birds may form large [flocks](/wiki/Flock_\(birds\) "Flock \(birds\)"). The principal benefits of flocking are [safety in numbers](/wiki/Safety_in_numbers "Safety in numbers") and increased foraging efficiency.[77] Defence against predators is particularly important in closed habitats like forests, where [ambush predation](/wiki/Ambush_predation "Ambush predation") is common and multiple eyes can provide a valuable [early warning system](/wiki/Early_warning_system "Early warning system"). This has led to the development of many [mixed-species feeding flocks](/wiki/Mixed-species_feeding_flock "Mixed-species feeding flock"), which are usually composed of small numbers of many species; these flocks provide safety in numbers but increase potential competition for resources.[197] Costs of flocking include bullying of socially subordinate birds by more dominant birds and the reduction of feeding efficiency in certain cases.[198] Some species have a mixed system with breeding pairs maintaining territories, while unmated or young birds live in flocks where they secure mates prior to finding territories.[199]

Birds sometimes also form associations with non-avian species. Plunge-diving [seabirds](/wiki/Seabird "Seabird") associate with [dolphins](/wiki/Dolphin "Dolphin") and [tuna](/wiki/Tuna "Tuna"), which push shoaling fish towards the surface.[200] Some species of [hornbills](/wiki/Hornbill "Hornbill") have a [mutualistic relationship](/wiki/Mutualism_\(biology\) "Mutualism \(biology\)") with [dwarf mongooses](/wiki/Dwarf_mongoose "Dwarf mongoose"), in which they forage together and warn each other of nearby [birds of prey](/wiki/Birds_of_prey "Birds of prey") and other predators.[201]

### Resting and roosting

"Roosting" redirects here. For other uses, see [Roost](/wiki/Roost_\(disambiguation\) "Roost \(disambiguation\)").

[](/wiki/File:Caribbean_Flamingo2_\(Phoenicopterus_ruber\)_\(0424\)_-_Relic38.jpg)Many birds, like this [American flamingo](/wiki/American_flamingo "American flamingo"), tuck their head into their back when sleeping.

The high metabolic rates of birds during the active part of the day is supplemented by rest at other times. [Sleeping birds](/wiki/Avian_sleep "Avian sleep") often use a type of sleep known as vigilant sleep, where periods of rest are interspersed with quick eye-opening "peeks", allowing them to be sensitive to disturbances and enable rapid escape from threats.[202] [Swifts](/wiki/Swift_\(bird\) "Swift \(bird\)") are believed to be able to sleep in flight and radar observations suggest that they orient themselves to face the wind in their roosting flight.[203] It has been suggested that there may be certain kinds of sleep which are possible even when in flight.[204]

Some birds have also demonstrated the capacity to fall into [slow-wave sleep](/wiki/Slow-wave_sleep "Slow-wave sleep") one [hemisphere](/wiki/Cerebral_hemisphere "Cerebral hemisphere") of the brain at a time. The birds tend to exercise this ability depending upon its position relative to the outside of the flock. This may allow the eye opposite the sleeping hemisphere to remain vigilant for [predators](/wiki/Predator "Predator") by viewing the outer margins of the flock. This adaptation is also known from [marine mammals](/wiki/Marine_mammal "Marine mammal").[205] [Communal roosting](/wiki/Communal_roosting "Communal roosting") is common because it lowers the [loss of body heat](/wiki/Thermoregulation "Thermoregulation") and decreases the risks associated with predators.[206] Roosting sites are often chosen with regard to thermoregulation and safety.[207] Unusual mobile roost sites include large herbivores on the African savanna that are used by [oxpeckers](/wiki/Oxpecker "Oxpecker").[208]

Many sleeping birds bend their heads over their backs and tuck their [bills](/wiki/Beak "Beak") in their back feathers, although others place their beaks among their breast feathers. Many birds rest on one leg, while some may pull up their legs into their feathers, especially in cold weather. [Perching birds](/wiki/Passerine "Passerine") have a tendon-locking mechanism that helps them hold on to the perch when they are asleep. Many ground birds, such as quails and pheasants, roost in trees. A few parrots of the genus _[Loriculus](/wiki/Loriculus "Loriculus")_ roost hanging upside down.[209] Some [hummingbirds](/wiki/Hummingbird "Hummingbird") go into a nightly state of [torpor](/wiki/Torpor "Torpor") accompanied with a reduction of their metabolic rates.[210] This [physiological adaptation](/wiki/Adaptation "Adaptation") shows in nearly a hundred other species, including [owlet-nightjars](/wiki/Owlet-nightjar "Owlet-nightjar"), [nightjars](/wiki/Nightjar "Nightjar"), and [woodswallows](/wiki/Woodswallow "Woodswallow"). One species, the [common poorwill](/wiki/Common_poorwill "Common poorwill"), even enters a state of [hibernation](/wiki/Hibernation "Hibernation").[211] Birds do not have sweat glands, but can lose water directly through the skin, and they may cool themselves by moving to shade, standing in water, panting, increasing their surface area, fluttering their throat or using special behaviours like [urohidrosis](/wiki/Urohidrosis "Urohidrosis") to cool themselves.[212][213]

### Breeding

See also: [Category:Avian sexuality](/wiki/Category:Avian_sexuality "Category:Avian sexuality"), [Animal sexual behaviour § Birds](/wiki/Animal_sexual_behaviour#Birds "Animal sexual behaviour"), [Seabird breeding behaviour](/wiki/Seabird_breeding_behaviour "Seabird breeding behaviour"), and [Sexual selection in birds](/wiki/Sexual_selection_in_birds "Sexual selection in birds")

#### Social systems

[](/wiki/File:Raggiana_Bird-of-Paradise_wild_5.jpg)Like others of its family, the male [Raggiana bird-of-paradise](/wiki/Raggiana_bird-of-paradise "Raggiana bird-of-paradise") has elaborate breeding plumage used to impress females.[214]

95 per cent of bird species are socially monogamous. These species pair for at least the length of the breeding season or—in some cases—for several years or until the death of one mate.[215] Monogamy allows for both [paternal care](/wiki/Paternal_care "Paternal care") and [biparental care](/wiki/Parental_investment "Parental investment"), which is especially important for species in which care from both the female and the male parent is required in order to successfully rear a brood.[216] Among many socially monogamous species, [extra-pair copulation](/wiki/Extra-pair_copulation "Extra-pair copulation") (infidelity) is common.[217] Such behaviour typically occurs between dominant males and females paired with subordinate males, but may also be the result of [forced copulation](/wiki/Forced_copulation "Forced copulation") in ducks and other [anatids](/wiki/Anatidae "Anatidae").[218]

For females, possible benefits of extra-pair copulation include getting better genes for her offspring and insuring against the possibility of infertility in her mate.[219] Males of species that engage in extra-pair copulations will closely guard their mates to ensure the parentage of the offspring that they raise.[220]

Other mating systems, including [polygyny](/wiki/Polygyny_in_animals "Polygyny in animals"), [polyandry](/wiki/Polyandry_in_animals "Polyandry in animals"), [polygamy](/wiki/Animal_sexual_behaviour#Polygamy "Animal sexual behaviour"), [polygynandry](/wiki/Polygynandry "Polygynandry"), and [promiscuity](/wiki/Promiscuity#Other_animals "Promiscuity"), also occur.[77] Polygamous breeding systems arise when females are able to raise broods without the help of males.[77] Mating systems vary across bird families[221] but variations within species are thought to be driven by environmental conditions.[222] A unique system is the formation of trios where a third individual is allowed by a breeding pair temporarily into the territory to assist with brood raising thereby leading to higher fitness.[223][190]

Breeding usually involves some form of courtship display, typically performed by the male.[224] Most displays are rather simple and involve some type of [song](/wiki/Bird_vocalization "Bird vocalization"). Some displays, however, are quite elaborate. Depending on the species, these may include wing or tail drumming, dancing, aerial flights, or communal [lekking](/wiki/Lek_mating "Lek mating"). Females are generally the ones that drive partner selection,[225] although in the polyandrous [phalaropes](/wiki/Phalaropes "Phalaropes"), this is reversed: plainer males choose brightly coloured females.[226] [Courtship feeding](/wiki/Courtship_feeding "Courtship feeding"), [billing](/wiki/Billing_\(birds\) "Billing \(birds\)") and [allopreening](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#allopreening "Glossary of bird terms") are commonly performed between partners, generally after the birds have paired and mated.[227]

[Homosexual behaviour has been observed](/wiki/Homosexuality_in_animals#Birds "Homosexuality in animals") in males or females in numerous species of birds, including copulation, pair-bonding, and joint parenting of chicks.[228] Over 130 avian species around the world engage in sexual interactions between the same sex or homosexual behaviours. "Same-sex courtship activities may involve elaborate displays, synchronised dances, gift-giving ceremonies, or behaviours at specific display areas including bowers, arenas, or leks."[229]

#### Territories, nesting and incubation

See also: [Bird nest](/wiki/Bird_nest "Bird nest")

[](/wiki/File:Bird-nest_\(2\).jpg)A bird nest which fell from a tree.

Many birds actively defend a territory from others of the same species during the breeding season; maintenance of territories protects the food source for their chicks. Species that are unable to defend feeding territories, such as [seabirds](/wiki/Seabird "Seabird") and [swifts](/wiki/Swift_\(bird\) "Swift \(bird\)"), often breed in [colonies](/wiki/Bird_colony "Bird colony") instead; this is thought to offer protection from predators. Colonial breeders defend small nesting sites, and competition between and within species for nesting sites can be intense.[230]

All birds lay [amniotic eggs](/wiki/Amniotic_egg "Amniotic egg") with hard shells made mostly of [calcium carbonate](/wiki/Calcium_carbonate "Calcium carbonate").[77] Hole and burrow nesting species tend to lay white or pale eggs, while open nesters lay [camouflaged](/wiki/Camouflage "Camouflage") eggs. There are many exceptions to this pattern, however; the ground-nesting [nightjars](/wiki/Nightjar "Nightjar") have pale eggs, and camouflage is instead provided by their plumage. Species that are victims of [brood parasites](/wiki/Brood_parasites "Brood parasites") have varying egg colours to improve the chances of spotting a parasite's egg, which forces female parasites to match their eggs to those of their hosts.[231]

[](/wiki/File:Golden-backed_Weaver.jpg)Male [golden-backed weavers](/wiki/Golden-backed_weaver "Golden-backed weaver") construct elaborate suspended nests out of grass.

Bird eggs are usually laid in a [nest](/wiki/Bird_nest "Bird nest"). Most species create somewhat elaborate nests, which can be cups, domes, plates, mounds, or burrows.[232] Some bird nests can be a simple scrape, with minimal or no lining; most seabird and wader nests are no more than a scrape on the ground. Most birds build nests in sheltered, hidden areas to avoid predation, but large or colonial birds—which are more capable of defence—may build more open nests. During nest construction, some species seek out plant matter from plants with parasite-reducing toxins to improve chick survival,[233] and feathers are often used for nest insulation.[232] Some bird species have no nests; the cliff-nesting [common guillemot](/wiki/Common_guillemot "Common guillemot") lays its eggs on bare rock, and male [emperor penguins](/wiki/Emperor_penguin "Emperor penguin") keep eggs between their body and feet. The absence of nests is especially prevalent in open habitat ground-nesting species where any addition of nest material would make the nest more conspicuous. Many ground nesting birds lay a clutch of eggs that hatch synchronously, with [precocial](/wiki/Precocial "Precocial") chicks led away from the nests ([nidifugous](/wiki/Nidifugous "Nidifugous")) by their parents soon after hatching.[234]

[](/wiki/File:Eastern_Phoebe-nest-Brown-headed-Cowbird-egg.jpg)Nest of an [eastern phoebe](/wiki/Eastern_phoebe "Eastern phoebe") that has been parasitised by a [brown-headed cowbird](/wiki/Brown-headed_cowbird "Brown-headed cowbird")

[Incubation](/wiki/Egg_incubation "Egg incubation"), which regulates temperature for chick development, usually begins after the last egg has been laid.[77] In monogamous species incubation duties are often shared, whereas in polygamous species one parent is wholly responsible for incubation. Warmth from parents passes to the eggs through [brood patches](/wiki/Brood_patch "Brood patch"), areas of bare skin on the abdomen or breast of the incubating birds. Incubation can be an energetically demanding process; adult albatrosses, for instance, lose as much as 83 grams (2.9 oz) of body weight per day of incubation.[235] The warmth for the incubation of the eggs of [megapodes](/wiki/Megapode "Megapode") comes from the sun, decaying vegetation or volcanic sources.[236] Incubation periods range from 10 days (in [woodpeckers](/wiki/Woodpecker "Woodpecker"), [cuckoos](/wiki/Cuckoo "Cuckoo") and [passerine](/wiki/Passerine "Passerine") birds) to over 80 days (in albatrosses and [kiwis](/wiki/Kiwi_\(bird\) "Kiwi \(bird\)")).[77]

The diversity of characteristics of birds is great, sometimes even in closely related species. Several avian characteristics are compared in the table below.[237][238]

Species  | Adult weight

[Ruby-throated hummingbird](/wiki/Ruby-throated_hummingbird "Ruby-throated hummingbird") (_Archilochus colubris_)  | 3  | 13  | 2.0  | 2
