#### Parental care and fledging

Main article: [Parental care in birds](/wiki/Parental_care_in_birds "Parental care in birds")

At the time of their hatching, chicks range in development from helpless to independent, depending on their species. Helpless chicks are termed _[altricial](/wiki/Altricial "Altricial")_ , and tend to be born small, [blind](/wiki/Blindness "Blindness"), immobile and naked; chicks that are mobile and feathered upon hatching are termed _[precocial](/wiki/Precocial "Precocial")_. Altricial chicks need help [thermoregulating](/wiki/Thermoregulation "Thermoregulation") and must be brooded for longer than precocial chicks. The young of many bird species do not precisely fit into either the precocial or altricial category, having some aspects of each and thus fall somewhere on an "altricial-precocial spectrum".[239] Chicks at neither extreme but favouring one or the other may be termed [semi-precocial](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#semi-precocial "Glossary of bird terms")[240] or [semi-altricial](/wiki/Glossary_of_bird_terms#semi-altricial "Glossary of bird terms").[241]

[](/wiki/File:White-breasted_Woodswallow_chicks_in_nest.jpg)[Altricial](/wiki/Altricial "Altricial") chicks of a [white-breasted woodswallow](/wiki/White-breasted_woodswallow "White-breasted woodswallow")

The length and nature of parental care varies widely amongst different orders and species. At one extreme, parental care in [megapodes](/wiki/Megapode "Megapode") ends at hatching; the newly hatched chick digs itself out of the nest mound without parental assistance and can fend for itself immediately.[242] At the other extreme, many seabirds have extended periods of parental care, the longest being that of the [great frigatebird](/wiki/Great_frigatebird "Great frigatebird"), whose chicks take up to six months to [fledge](/wiki/Fledge "Fledge") and are fed by the parents for up to an additional 14 months.[243] The _chick guard stage_ describes the period of breeding during which one of the adult birds is permanently present at the nest after chicks have hatched. The main purpose of the guard stage is to aid offspring to thermoregulate and protect them from predation.[244]

[](/wiki/File:Calliope-nest_edit.jpg)A female [calliope hummingbird](/wiki/Calliope_hummingbird "Calliope hummingbird") feeding fully grown chicks

In some species, both parents care for nestlings and fledglings; in others, such care is the responsibility of only one sex. In some species, [other members](/wiki/Helpers_at_the_nest "Helpers at the nest") of the same species—usually close relatives of the [breeding pair](/wiki/Breeding_pair "Breeding pair"), such as offspring from previous broods—will help with the raising of the young.[245] Such [alloparenting](/wiki/Alloparenting "Alloparenting") is particularly common among the [Corvida](/wiki/Corvida "Corvida"), which includes such birds as the true [crows](/wiki/Corvidae "Corvidae"), [Australian magpie](/wiki/Australian_magpie "Australian magpie") and [fairy-wrens](/wiki/Fairy-wren "Fairy-wren"),[246] but has been observed in species as different as the [rifleman](/wiki/Rifleman_\(bird\) "Rifleman \(bird\)") and [red kite](/wiki/Red_kite "Red kite"). Among most groups of animals, [male parental care](/wiki/Paternal_care "Paternal care") is rare. In birds, however, it is quite common—more so than in any other vertebrate class.[77] Although territory and nest site defence, incubation, and chick feeding are often shared tasks, there is sometimes a [division of labour](/wiki/Division_of_labour "Division of labour") in which one mate undertakes all or most of a particular duty.[247]

The point at which chicks [fledge](/wiki/Fledge "Fledge") varies dramatically. The chicks of the _[Synthliboramphus](/wiki/Synthliboramphus "Synthliboramphus")_ murrelets, like the [ancient murrelet](/wiki/Ancient_murrelet "Ancient murrelet"), leave the nest the night after they hatch, following their parents out to sea, where they are raised away from terrestrial predators.[248] Some other species, such as ducks, move their chicks away from the nest at an early age. In most species, chicks leave the nest just before, or soon after, they are able to fly. The amount of parental care after fledging varies; albatross chicks leave the nest on their own and receive no further help, while other species continue some supplementary feeding after fledging.[249] Chicks may also follow their parents during their first [migration](/wiki/Bird_migration "Bird migration").[250]

#### Brood parasites

Main article: [Brood parasite](/wiki/Brood_parasite "Brood parasite")

[](/wiki/File:Reed_warbler_cuckoo.jpg)[Reed warbler](/wiki/Reed_warbler "Reed warbler") raising a [common cuckoo](/wiki/Common_cuckoo "Common cuckoo"), a [brood parasite](/wiki/Brood_parasite "Brood parasite")

[Brood parasitism](/wiki/Brood_parasitism "Brood parasitism"), in which an egg-layer leaves her eggs with another individual's brood, is more common among birds than any other type of organism.[251] After a parasitic bird lays her eggs in another bird's nest, they are often accepted and raised by the host at the expense of the host's own brood. Brood parasites may be either _obligate brood parasites_ , which must lay their eggs in the nests of other species because they are incapable of raising their own young, or _non-obligate brood parasites_ , which sometimes lay eggs in the nests of [conspecifics](/wiki/Conspecific "Conspecific") to increase their reproductive output even though they could have raised their own young.[252] One hundred bird species, including [honeyguides](/wiki/Honeyguide "Honeyguide"), [icterids](/wiki/Icterid "Icterid"), and [ducks](/wiki/Black-headed_duck "Black-headed duck"), are obligate parasites, though the most famous are the [cuckoos](/wiki/Cuckoo "Cuckoo").[251] Some brood parasites are adapted to hatch before their host's young, which allows them to destroy the host's eggs by pushing them out of the nest or to kill the host's chicks; this ensures that all food brought to the nest will be fed to the parasitic chicks.[253]

#### Sexual selection

[](/wiki/File:Peacock_Flying.jpg)The peacock tail in flight, the classic example of a [Fisherian runaway](/wiki/Fisherian_runaway "Fisherian runaway")

Main article: [Sexual selection in birds](/wiki/Sexual_selection_in_birds "Sexual selection in birds")

Birds have [evolved](/wiki/Evolution "Evolution") a variety of [mating](/wiki/Mating "Mating") behaviours, with the [peacock](/wiki/Peafowl "Peafowl") tail being perhaps the most famous example of [sexual selection](/wiki/Sexual_selection "Sexual selection") and the [Fisherian runaway](/wiki/Fisherian_runaway "Fisherian runaway"). Commonly occurring [sexual dimorphisms](/wiki/Sexual_dimorphism "Sexual dimorphism") such as size and colour differences are energetically costly attributes that signal competitive breeding situations.[254] Many types of avian [sexual selection](/wiki/Sexual_selection "Sexual selection") have been identified; intersexual selection, also known as female choice; and intrasexual competition, where individuals of the more abundant sex compete with each other for the privilege to mate. Sexually selected traits often evolve to become more pronounced in competitive breeding situations until the trait begins to limit the individual's fitness. Conflicts between an individual fitness and signalling adaptations ensure that sexually selected ornaments such as plumage colouration and [courtship behaviour](/wiki/Courtship_behavior "Courtship behavior") are "honest" traits. Signals must be costly to ensure that only good-quality individuals can present these exaggerated sexual ornaments and behaviours.[255]

#### Inbreeding depression

Main article: [Inbreeding depression](/wiki/Inbreeding_depression "Inbreeding depression")

[Inbreeding](/wiki/Inbreeding "Inbreeding") causes early death ([inbreeding depression](/wiki/Inbreeding_depression "Inbreeding depression")) in the [zebra finch](/wiki/Zebra_finch "Zebra finch") _Taeniopygia guttata_.[256] Embryo survival (that is, hatching success of fertile eggs) was significantly lower for [sib-sib](/wiki/Sibling "Sibling") mating pairs than for unrelated pairs.[257]

[Darwin's finch](/wiki/Darwin%27s_finches "Darwin's finches") _Geospiza scandens_ experiences [inbreeding depression](/wiki/Inbreeding_depression "Inbreeding depression") (reduced survival of offspring) and the magnitude of this effect is influenced by environmental conditions such as low food availability.[258]

#### Inbreeding avoidance

Main article: [Inbreeding avoidance](/wiki/Inbreeding_avoidance "Inbreeding avoidance")

Incestuous matings by the [purple-crowned fairy wren](/wiki/Purple-crowned_fairywren "Purple-crowned fairywren") _Malurus coronatus_ result in severe fitness costs due to [inbreeding depression](/wiki/Inbreeding_depression "Inbreeding depression") (greater than 30% reduction in hatchability of eggs).[259] Females paired with related males may undertake extra pair matings (see [Promiscuity#Other animals](/wiki/Promiscuity#Other_animals "Promiscuity") for 90% frequency in avian species) that can reduce the negative effects of inbreeding. However, there are ecological and demographic constraints on extra pair matings. Nevertheless, 43% of broods produced by incestuously paired females contained extra pair young.[259]

Inbreeding depression occurs in the [great tit](/wiki/Great_tit "Great tit") (_Parus major_) when the offspring produced as a result of a mating between close relatives show reduced fitness. In natural populations of _Parus major_ , inbreeding is avoided by dispersal of individuals from their birthplace, which reduces the chance of mating with a close relative.[260]

[Southern pied babblers](/wiki/Southern_pied_babbler "Southern pied babbler") _Turdoides bicolor_ appear to avoid inbreeding in two ways. The first is through dispersal, and the second is by avoiding familiar group members as mates.[261]

[Cooperative breeding](/wiki/Cooperative_breeding "Cooperative breeding") in birds typically occurs when offspring, usually males, delay dispersal from their natal group in order to remain with the family to help rear younger kin.[262] Female offspring rarely stay at home, dispersing over distances that allow them to breed independently, or to join unrelated groups. In general, inbreeding is avoided because it leads to a reduction in progeny fitness ([inbreeding depression](/wiki/Inbreeding_depression "Inbreeding depression")) due largely to the homozygous expression of deleterious recessive alleles.[263] [Cross-fertilisation](/wiki/Outcrossing "Outcrossing") between unrelated individuals ordinarily leads to the masking of deleterious recessive alleles in progeny.[264][265]

## Ecology

[](/wiki/File:Pinz%C3%B3n_azul_de_Gran_Canaria_\(macho\),_M._A._Pe%C3%B1a.jpg)[Gran Canaria blue chaffinch](/wiki/Gran_Canaria_blue_chaffinch "Gran Canaria blue chaffinch"), an example of a bird highly specialised in its habitat, in this case in the [Canarian pine](/wiki/Pinus_canariensis "Pinus canariensis") forests

Birds occupy a wide range of ecological positions.[196] While some birds are generalists, others are highly specialised in their habitat or food requirements. Even within a single habitat, such as a forest, the [niches](/wiki/Ecological_niche "Ecological niche") occupied by different species of birds vary, with some species feeding in the [forest canopy](/wiki/Forest_canopy "Forest canopy"), others beneath the canopy, and still others on the forest floor. Forest birds may be [insectivores](/wiki/Insectivore "Insectivore"), [frugivores](/wiki/Frugivore "Frugivore"), or [nectarivores](/wiki/Nectarivore "Nectarivore"). Aquatic birds generally feed by fishing, plant eating, and piracy or [kleptoparasitism](/wiki/Kleptoparasitism "Kleptoparasitism"). Many grassland birds are granivores. Birds of prey specialise in hunting mammals or other birds, while vultures are specialised [scavengers](/wiki/Scavenger "Scavenger"). Birds are also preyed upon by a range of mammals including a few [avivorous](/wiki/Avivore "Avivore") bats.[266] A wide range of endo- and ectoparasites depend on birds and some parasites that are transmitted from parent to young have [co-evolved](/wiki/Coevolution "Coevolution") and show host-specificity.[267]

Some nectar-feeding birds are important pollinators, and many frugivores play a key role in seed dispersal.[268] Plants and pollinating birds often [coevolve](/wiki/Coevolution "Coevolution"),[269] and in some cases a flower's primary pollinator is the only species capable of reaching its nectar.[270]

Birds are often important to island ecology. Birds have frequently reached islands that mammals have not; on those islands, birds may fulfil ecological roles typically played by larger animals. For example, in New Zealand nine species of [moa](/wiki/Moa "Moa") were important browsers, as are the [kererū](/wiki/Kerer%C5%AB "Kererū") and [kōkako](/wiki/K%C5%8Dkako "Kōkako") today.[268] Today the plants of New Zealand retain the defensive adaptations evolved to protect them from the extinct moa.[271]

Many birds act as [ecosystem engineers](/wiki/Ecosystem_engineer "Ecosystem engineer") through the construction of nests, which provide important microhabitats and food for hundreds of species of invertebrates.[272][273] Nesting [seabirds](/wiki/Seabird "Seabird") may affect the ecology of islands and surrounding seas, principally through the concentration of large quantities of [guano](/wiki/Guano "Guano"), which may enrich the local soil[274] and the surrounding seas.[275]

A wide variety of [avian ecology field methods](/wiki/Avian_ecology_field_methods "Avian ecology field methods"), including counts, nest monitoring, and capturing and marking, are used for researching avian ecology.[276]

## Relationship with humans

Main article: [Human uses of birds](/wiki/Human_uses_of_birds "Human uses of birds")

[](/wiki/File:Industrial-Chicken-Coop.JPG)[Industrial farming](/wiki/Industrial_farming "Industrial farming") of [chickens](/wiki/Chicken "Chicken")

Since birds are highly visible and common animals, humans have had a relationship with them since the dawn of man.[277] Sometimes, these relationships are [mutualistic](/wiki/Mutualism_\(biology\) "Mutualism \(biology\)"), like the cooperative honey-gathering among [honeyguides](/wiki/Honeyguide "Honeyguide") and African peoples such as the [Borana](/wiki/Borana_people "Borana people").[278] Other times, they may be [commensal](/wiki/Commensalism "Commensalism"), as when species such as the [house sparrow](/wiki/House_sparrow "House sparrow")[279] have benefited from human activities. Several species have reconciled to habits of farmers who practice traditional farming. Examples include the [Sarus Crane](/wiki/Sarus_crane "Sarus crane") that begins nesting in India when farmers flood the fields in anticipation of rains,[280] and the [woolly-necked storks](/wiki/Asian_woolly-necked_stork "Asian woolly-necked stork") that have taken to nesting on a short tree grown for agroforestry beside fields and canals.[281] Several bird species have become commercially significant agricultural pests,[282] and some pose an [aviation hazard](/wiki/Bird_strike "Bird strike").[283] Human activities can also be detrimental, and have threatened numerous bird species with extinction ([hunting](/wiki/Hunting "Hunting"), [avian lead poisoning](/wiki/Avian_lead_poisoning "Avian lead poisoning"), [pesticides](/wiki/Pesticide "Pesticide"), [roadkill](/wiki/Roadkill "Roadkill"), [wind turbine](/wiki/Wind_turbine "Wind turbine") kills[284] and predation by pet [cats](/wiki/Cat "Cat") and [dogs](/wiki/Dog "Dog") are common causes of death for birds).[285]

Birds can act as vectors for spreading diseases such as [psittacosis](/wiki/Psittacosis "Psittacosis"), [salmonellosis](/wiki/Salmonellosis "Salmonellosis"), [campylobacteriosis](/wiki/Campylobacteriosis "Campylobacteriosis"), mycobacteriosis (avian [tuberculosis](/wiki/Tuberculosis "Tuberculosis")), [avian influenza](/wiki/Avian_influenza "Avian influenza") (bird flu), [giardiasis](/wiki/Giardiasis "Giardiasis"), and [cryptosporidiosis](/wiki/Cryptosporidiosis "Cryptosporidiosis") over long distances. Some of these are [zoonotic diseases](/wiki/Zoonosis "Zoonosis") that can also be transmitted to humans.[286]

### Economic importance

See also: [Pet § Birds](/wiki/Pet#Birds "Pet")

[](/wiki/File:FishingCormorants.jpg)The use of cormorants by Asian fishermen is in steep decline but survives in some areas as a tourist attraction.

Domesticated birds raised for meat and eggs, called [poultry](/wiki/Poultry "Poultry"), are the largest source of animal protein eaten by humans; in 2003, 76 million tons of poultry and 61 million tons of eggs were produced worldwide.[287] [Chickens](/wiki/Chicken "Chicken") account for much of human poultry consumption, though domesticated [turkeys](/wiki/Domesticated_turkey "Domesticated turkey"), [ducks](/wiki/Domestic_duck "Domestic duck"), and [geese](/wiki/Domestic_goose "Domestic goose") are also relatively common.[288] Many species of birds are also hunted for meat. Bird hunting is primarily a recreational activity except in extremely undeveloped areas. The most important birds hunted in North and South America are waterfowl; other widely hunted birds include [pheasants](/wiki/Pheasant "Pheasant"), [wild turkeys](/wiki/Wild_turkey "Wild turkey"), quail, [doves](/wiki/Dove "Dove"), [partridge](/wiki/Partridge "Partridge"), [grouse](/wiki/Grouse "Grouse"), [snipe](/wiki/Snipe "Snipe"), and [woodcock](/wiki/Woodcock "Woodcock").[289] [Muttonbirding](/wiki/Muttonbirding "Muttonbirding") is also popular in Australia and New Zealand.[290] Although some hunting, such as that of muttonbirds, may be sustainable, hunting has led to the extinction or endangerment of dozens of species.[291]

Other commercially valuable products from birds include feathers (especially the [down](/wiki/Down_feather "Down feather") of geese and ducks), which are used as insulation in clothing and bedding, and seabird faeces ([guano](/wiki/Guano "Guano")), which is a valuable source of phosphorus and nitrogen. The [War of the Pacific](/wiki/War_of_the_Pacific "War of the Pacific"), sometimes called the Guano War, was fought in part over the control of guano deposits.[292]

Birds have been domesticated by humans both as pets and for practical purposes. Colourful birds, such as [parrots](/wiki/Parrot_\(family\) "Parrot \(family\)") and [mynas](/wiki/Myna "Myna"), are bred in [captivity](/wiki/Aviculture "Aviculture") or kept as pets, a practice that has led to the illegal trafficking of some [endangered species](/wiki/Endangered_species "Endangered species").[293] [Falcons](/wiki/Falcon "Falcon") and [cormorants](/wiki/Cormorant "Cormorant") have long been used for [hunting](/wiki/Falconry "Falconry") and [fishing](/wiki/Cormorant_fishing "Cormorant fishing"), respectively. [Messenger pigeons](/wiki/Messenger_pigeon "Messenger pigeon"), used since at least 1 AD, remained important as recently as [World War II](/wiki/World_War_II "World War II"). Today, such activities are more common either as hobbies, for entertainment and tourism.[294]

Amateur bird enthusiasts (called birdwatchers, twitchers or, more commonly, [birders](/wiki/Birding "Birding")) number in the millions.[295] Many homeowners erect [bird feeders](/wiki/Bird_feeder "Bird feeder") near their homes to attract various species. [Bird feeding](/wiki/Bird_feeding "Bird feeding") has grown into a multimillion-dollar industry; for example, an estimated 75% of households in Britain provide food for birds at some point during the winter.[296]

### In religion and mythology

[](/wiki/File:Vogel_Drei_\(Meister_der_Spielkarten\).jpg)The _3 of Birds_ by the [Master of the Playing Cards](/wiki/Master_of_the_Playing_Cards "Master of the Playing Cards"), 15th-century Germany

Birds play prominent and diverse roles in religion and mythology. In religion, birds may serve as either messengers or priests and leaders for a [deity](/wiki/Deity "Deity"), such as in the Cult of [Makemake](/wiki/Makemake_\(mythology\) "Makemake \(mythology\)"), in which the [Tangata manu](/wiki/Tangata_manu "Tangata manu") of [Easter Island](/wiki/Easter_Island "Easter Island") served as chiefs[297] or as attendants, as in the case of [Hugin and Munin](/wiki/Hugin_and_Munin "Hugin and Munin"), the two [common ravens](/wiki/Common_raven "Common raven") who whispered news into the ears of the [Norse god](/wiki/Norse_god "Norse god") [Odin](/wiki/Odin "Odin"). In several civilisations of [ancient Italy](/wiki/History_of_Italy "History of Italy"), particularly [Etruscan](/wiki/Etruscan_mythology "Etruscan mythology") and [Roman](/wiki/Ancient_Rome "Ancient Rome") [religion](/wiki/Religion_in_Ancient_Rome "Religion in Ancient Rome"), priests were involved in [augury](/wiki/Augur "Augur"), or interpreting the words of birds while the "auspex" (from which the word "auspicious" is derived) watched their activities to foretell events.[298]

They may also serve as [religious symbols](/wiki/Religious_symbolism "Religious symbolism"), as when [Jonah](/wiki/Jonah "Jonah") ([Hebrew](/wiki/Hebrew_language "Hebrew language"): יונה, [dove](/wiki/Dove "Dove")) embodied the fright, passivity, mourning, and beauty traditionally associated with doves.[299] Birds have themselves been deified, as in the case of the [common peacock](/wiki/Common_peacock "Common peacock"), which is perceived as Mother Earth by the people of southern India.[300] In the ancient world, doves were used as symbols of the [Mesopotamian goddess](/wiki/Mesopotamian_goddess "Mesopotamian goddess") [Inanna](/wiki/Inanna "Inanna") (later known as Ishtar),[301][302] the [Canaanite](/wiki/Ancient_Canaanite_religion "Ancient Canaanite religion") mother goddess [Asherah](/wiki/Asherah "Asherah"),[301][302][303] and the Greek goddess [Aphrodite](/wiki/Aphrodite "Aphrodite").[301][302][304][305][306] In [ancient Greece](/wiki/Ancient_Greece "Ancient Greece"), [Athena](/wiki/Athena "Athena"), the goddess of wisdom and patron deity of the city of [Athens](/wiki/Athens "Athens"), had a [little owl](/wiki/Little_owl "Little owl") as [her symbol](/wiki/Owl_of_Athena "Owl of Athena").[307][308][309] In religious images preserved from the Inca and Tiwanaku empires, birds are depicted in the process of transgressing boundaries between earthly and underground spiritual realms.[310] Indigenous peoples of the central Andes maintain legends of birds passing to and from metaphysical worlds.[310]

### In culture and folklore

Further information: [Birds in culture](/wiki/Birds_in_culture "Birds in culture")

[](/wiki/File:17.6-24-1974-Cuerda-seca-flisepanel.jpg)Painted [tiles](/wiki/Tiles "Tiles") with design of birds from [Qajar dynasty](/wiki/Qajar_dynasty "Qajar dynasty"), [Iran](/wiki/Iran "Iran")

Birds have featured in culture and art since prehistoric times, when they were represented in early [cave painting](/wiki/Cave_painting "Cave painting")[311] and carvings.[312] Some birds have been perceived as monsters, including the mythological [Roc](/wiki/Roc_\(mythology\) "Roc \(mythology\)") and the [Māori](/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people "Māori people")'s legendary _[Pouākai](/wiki/Poukai "Poukai")_ , a giant bird capable of snatching humans.[313] Birds were later used as symbols of power, as in the magnificent [Peacock Throne](/wiki/Peacock_Throne "Peacock Throne") of the [Mughal](/wiki/Mughal_era "Mughal era") and [Persian](/wiki/History_of_Iran "History of Iran") emperors.[314] With the advent of scientific interest in birds, many paintings of birds were commissioned for books.[315][316]

Among the most famous of these bird artists was [John James Audubon](/wiki/John_James_Audubon "John James Audubon"), whose paintings of [North American birds](/wiki/List_of_North_American_birds "List of North American birds") were a great commercial success in Europe and who later lent his name to the [National Audubon Society](/wiki/National_Audubon_Society "National Audubon Society").[317] Birds are also important figures in poetry; for example, [Homer](/wiki/Homer "Homer") incorporated [nightingales](/wiki/Nightingale "Nightingale") into his _[Odyssey](/wiki/Odyssey "Odyssey")_ , and [Catullus](/wiki/Catullus "Catullus") used a [sparrow](/wiki/Old_World_sparrow "Old World sparrow") as an erotic symbol in his [Catullus 2](/wiki/Catullus_2 "Catullus 2").[318] The relationship between an [albatross](/wiki/Albatross "Albatross") and a sailor is the central theme of [Samuel Taylor Coleridge](/wiki/Samuel_Taylor_Coleridge "Samuel Taylor Coleridge")'s _[The Rime of the Ancient Mariner](/wiki/The_Rime_of_the_Ancient_Mariner "The Rime of the Ancient Mariner")_ , which led to the use of the [term as a metaphor for a 'burden'](/wiki/Albatross_\(metaphor\) "Albatross \(metaphor\)").[319] Other [English](/wiki/English_language "English language") metaphors derive from birds; [vulture funds](/wiki/Vulture_fund "Vulture fund") and vulture investors, for instance, take their name from the scavenging vulture.[320] Aircraft, particularly military aircraft, are frequently named after birds. The predatory nature of raptors make them popular choices for fighter aircraft such as the [F-16 Fighting Falcon](/wiki/F-16_Fighting_Falcon "F-16 Fighting Falcon") and the [Harrier Jump Jet](/wiki/Harrier_Jump_Jet "Harrier Jump Jet"), while the names of seabirds may be chosen for aircraft primarily used by naval forces such as the [HU-16 Albatross](/wiki/HU-16_Albatross "HU-16 Albatross") and the [V-22 Osprey](/wiki/V-22_Osprey "V-22 Osprey"). 

[](/wiki/File:Flag_of_Dominica.svg)The [flag](/wiki/Flag_of_Dominica "Flag of Dominica") of [Dominica](/wiki/Dominica "Dominica") prominently features the [Sisserou Parrot](/wiki/Sisserou_Parrot "Sisserou Parrot"), its national bird.

Perceptions of bird species vary across cultures. [Owls](/wiki/Owl "Owl") are associated with bad luck, [witchcraft](/wiki/Witchcraft "Witchcraft"), and death in parts of Africa,[321] but are regarded as wise across much of Europe.[322] [Hoopoes](/wiki/Hoopoe "Hoopoe") were considered sacred in [Ancient Egypt](/wiki/Ancient_Egypt "Ancient Egypt") and symbols of virtue in [Persia](/wiki/Persia "Persia"), but were thought of as thieves across much of Europe and harbingers of war in [Scandinavia](/wiki/Scandinavia "Scandinavia").[323] In [heraldry](/wiki/Heraldry "Heraldry"), birds, especially [eagles](/wiki/Eagle_\(heraldry\) "Eagle \(heraldry\)"), often appear in [coats of arms](/wiki/Coats_of_arms "Coats of arms")[324] In [vexillology](/wiki/Vexillology "Vexillology"), birds are a popular choice on [flags](/wiki/List_of_national_flags_by_design#Birds "List of national flags by design"). Birds feature in the flag designs of 17 countries and numerous subnational entities and territories.[325] Birds are used by nations to symbolise a country's identity and heritage, with 91 countries officially recognising a [national bird](/wiki/List_of_national_birds "List of national birds"). Birds of prey are highly represented, though some nations have chosen other species of birds with parrots being popular among smaller, tropical nations.[326]

### In music

Main article: [Birds in music](/wiki/Birds_in_music "Birds in music")

[In music](/wiki/Birds_in_music "Birds in music"), birdsong has influenced composers and musicians in several ways: they can be inspired by birdsong; they can intentionally imitate bird song in a composition, as [Vivaldi](/wiki/Antonio_Vivaldi "Antonio Vivaldi"), [Messiaen](/wiki/Olivier_Messiaen "Olivier Messiaen"), and [Beethoven](/wiki/Beethoven "Beethoven") did, along with many later composers; they can incorporate recordings of birds into their works, as [Ottorino Respighi](/wiki/Ottorino_Respighi "Ottorino Respighi") first did; or like [Beatrice Harrison](/wiki/Beatrice_Harrison "Beatrice Harrison") and [David Rothenberg](/wiki/David_Rothenberg "David Rothenberg"), they can duet with birds.[327][328][329][330]

A 2023 archaeological excavation of a 10,000-year-old site in Israel yielded hollow wing bones of coots and ducks with perforations made on the side that are thought to have allowed them to be used as flutes or whistles possibly used by [Natufian](/wiki/Natufian_culture "Natufian culture") people to lure birds of prey.[331]

## Threats and conservation

Main article: [Bird conservation](/wiki/Bird_conservation "Bird conservation")

See also: [Late Quaternary prehistoric birds](/wiki/Late_Quaternary_prehistoric_birds "Late Quaternary prehistoric birds"), [List of extinct birds](/wiki/List_of_extinct_birds "List of extinct birds"), and [Raptor conservation](/wiki/Raptor_conservation "Raptor conservation")

[](/wiki/File:California-Condor3-Szmurlo_edit.jpg)The [California condor](/wiki/California_condor "California condor") once numbered only 22 birds, but conservation measures have raised that to over 500 today.

Human activities have caused population decreases or [extinction](/wiki/Extinction "Extinction") in many bird species. Over a hundred bird species have gone extinct in historical times,[332] although the most dramatic human-caused avian extinctions, eradicating an estimated 750–1800 species, occurred during the human colonisation of [Melanesian](/wiki/Melanesia "Melanesia"), [Polynesian](/wiki/Polynesia "Polynesia"), and [Micronesian](/wiki/Micronesia "Micronesia") islands.[333] Many bird populations are declining worldwide, with 1,227 species listed as [threatened](/wiki/Threatened_species "Threatened species") by [BirdLife International](/wiki/BirdLife_International "BirdLife International") and the [IUCN](/wiki/IUCN "IUCN") in 2009.[334][335] There have been long-term declines in North American bird populations, with an estimated loss of 2.9 billion breeding adults, about 30% of the total, since 1970.[336][337]

The most commonly cited human threat to birds is [habitat loss](/wiki/Habitat_destruction "Habitat destruction").[338] Other threats include overhunting, accidental mortality due to collisions with [buildings](/wiki/Bird-skyscraper_collisions "Bird-skyscraper collisions") or [vehicles](/wiki/Bird_strike "Bird strike"), [long-line fishing](/wiki/Long-line_fishing "Long-line fishing") [bycatch](/wiki/Bycatch "Bycatch"),[339] pollution (including [oil spills](/wiki/Oil_spill "Oil spill") and pesticide use),[340] competition and predation from nonnative [invasive species](/wiki/Invasive_species "Invasive species"),[341] and [climate change](/wiki/Climate_change_and_birds "Climate change and birds"). 

Governments and [conservation](/wiki/Conservation_biology "Conservation biology") groups work to protect birds, either by passing laws that [preserve](/wiki/In-situ_conservation "In-situ conservation") and [restore](/wiki/Ecological_restoration "Ecological restoration") bird habitat or by establishing [captive populations](/wiki/Ex-situ_conservation "Ex-situ conservation") for reintroductions. Such projects have produced some successes; one study estimated that conservation efforts saved 16 species of bird that would otherwise have gone extinct between 1994 and 2004, including the [California condor](/wiki/California_condor "California condor") and [Norfolk parakeet](/wiki/Norfolk_parakeet "Norfolk parakeet").[342]

Human activities have allowed the expansion of a few temperate area species, such as the [barn swallow](/wiki/Barn_swallow "Barn swallow") and [European starling](/wiki/European_starling "European starling"). In the tropics and sub-tropics, relatively more species are expanding due to human activities, particularly due to the spread of crops such as rice whose expansion in south Asia has benefitted at least 64 bird species, though may have harmed many more species.[343]
